INTRODUCTION
Who This Is For
This prompt pack was written for people who already know networking — and want to use AI to
work faster and better, not to replace their judgment.
Specifically, you'll get the most value from this pack if you are:
A network engineer or network administrator managing multi-VLAN environments with
enterprise or SMB-grade switches, firewalls, and wireless infrastructure
A systems administrator or IT consultant who handles the full stack — from cable to cloud —
for one or more organizations
A developer or DevOps engineer exploring automation, scripting, and agentic AI workflows
for IT infrastructure
A network architecture student or junior engineer who wants to see how professionals apply
AI as a structured thinking tool — not a magic answer machine
You should have working knowledge of: VLANs, IP subnetting, firewall policies, basic CLI, and the
concept of AI prompt engineering. You don't need to be an expert in all of these — but you do
need to know enough to recognize a bad AI output when you see one.
Who This Is NOT For
Be honest with yourself about whether this is the right product:
Absolute networking beginners — if you're still learning what a VLAN is, the prompts in
Categories 1–4 will produce output you can't safely evaluate. Learn the fundamentals first,
then come back.
Anyone expecting copy-paste configurations without review — AI-generated network
configurations and scripts will contain mistakes. This pack is designed to accelerate your
workflow, not eliminate professional judgment.
Teams operating in highly regulated environments (HIPAA, PCI DSS, FedRAMP, etc.) without
an additional compliance review layer — the prompts provide general guidance; regulated
environments require additional controls not covered here.
Individuals planning to test networks they do not own or have not been authorized to test
— see the Legal Notice above.
What Makes This Pack Different
Most AI prompt packs are glorified ChatGPT conversation starters. This one is a professional-
grade tool with:
50 full-depth, 9-field prompts (not 5-field abbreviated placeholders). Every prompt includes
context, required inputs, expected output, the prompt itself, a worked example with realistic
sample output, variations, safety warnings, and a difficulty rating.

A consistent canonical reference network throughout all worked examples. Every device, IP
address, VLAN ID, and hostname in every example is the same — so you can follow worked
examples across prompts and see how they interconnect.
Realistic sample outputs — not descriptions of what the output would be, but actual abridged
tables, CLI snippets, and structured text you can compare against your own AI results.
Agentic AI coverage with practical safety controls — prompt injection defenses, HITL gates, kill
switches, and cost limits baked into every agent template.
Python 3.11+ automation prompts with dry-run flags, test-one-device-first logic, environment-
variable-based secrets, and error handling — not toy scripts.
US/UK legal framing — CFAA, Computer Misuse Act 1990, UK GDPR/ICO, NIST CSF 2.0, CIS
Controls v8.1. No Italian-specific law, no misattributed statutes.
[VERIFY] transparency — where source materials were unavailable for verification at writing time,
items are flagged with the exact source needed. Stale data is acknowledged, not papered over.

HOW TO USE THIS PACK
Five Principles for High-Quality AI Output
Principle 1 — Always Provide Specific Context
An AI model doesn't know your network. Output quality is directly proportional to input quality.
Replace every [PLACEHOLDER] with your actual data: device models, firmware versions, IP ranges,
VLAN IDs, device counts, and special requirements. A generic prompt gets generic output. A
prompt filled with your specific infrastructure data gets actionable, applicable output.
Principle 2 — Specify Your Output Format
If you need a table, say "respond with a table." If you need CLI commands ready to paste, say
"CLI commands only, no explanation." If you need a step-by-step procedure, say "numbered
steps." Each prompt already specifies a default format where relevant — but you can always
override it. Adding format instructions costs nothing and dramatically improves output usability.
Principle 3 — Iterate and Refine
The first output is rarely the final one. After the initial response, add:
- "Expand on point 3"
- "Reformat as CLI commands for FortiOS 7.4"
- "Add error handling for the case where the device is unreachable"
- "Adapt this for Cisco IOS-XE instead of D-Link"
AI is a conversation partner, not a one-shot vending machine. Multi-turn conversations produce
progressively better, more tailored output.
Principle 4 — Always Verify Technical Output
Configurations, scripts, and security policies generated by AI must be tested in a lab or staging
environment before production deployment. No AI model is free of technical errors — wrong CLI
syntax, incorrect DHCP ranges, incomplete ACL rules, and outdated security practices all appear
regularly in AI-generated network content. Your professional judgment is not optional. It is the
point.
Principle 5 — Protect Sensitive Data
Do not paste real public IP addresses, passwords, private keys, API tokens, or identifying
organizational data into cloud AI services. Use the reference network values provided in this
pack, or substitute fictional values, then adapt the AI's output to your real environment. Before
sharing any infrastructure data with an AI service, verify that provider's current data handling and
retention policies at their official documentation. Retention policies change — do not rely on
memory or third-party summaries.
📅  AI Model Guidance (as of Q3 2026)
Current frontier models with long context windows and strong technical reasoning perform best with
these prompts. As of mid-2026, this category includes GPT-4o-class models (OpenAI), Claude
3.7/4-class models (Anthropic), and Gemini 2.x-class models (Google). For on-premises use

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition

without data leaving your network: Llama 3.x 70B+ or equivalent large local models via Ollama are
viable for structured prompts with shorter contexts.
Model capabilities, names, and availability change rapidly. Verify current model availability, pricing,
and context window sizes at the provider's official documentation before selecting a model for
production workflows. Do not rely on version names or context window figures quoted in third-party
sources (including this pack) without checking the primary source.
For maximum performance on long prompts (Categories 5–7), prefer models with 100K+ context
windows. For Categories 1–4, mid-tier models with 32K+ contexts work well. [VERIFY: check current
context window sizes at platform.openai.com/docs, docs.anthropic.com, and ai.google.dev before
purchasing API access.]

PRE-FLIGHT CHECKLIST
Complete this checklist before running any prompt in this pack. Each unchecked item is a
potential risk to output quality, operational safety, or compliance.
[ ] I have read all five principles above and understand how AI output quality depends on input
quality
[ ] I have identified every [PLACEHOLDER] in the prompt and know exactly what value to
substitute
[ ] I have removed or anonymized all sensitive data: real passwords, real public IPs, private
keys, credentials, personally identifiable information
[ ] I have chosen an AI model appropriate for the task complexity (see Model Guidance box
above)
[ ] For network configurations: I have a lab, test device, or staging environment ready before
applying any output to production
[ ] For automation scripts: I understand the --dry-run and --device flags provided in every
Category 6 prompt, and I will use them
[ ] For security assessments and port scanning: I have written authorization from the
network owner before proceeding

before distributing

submitted data

QUICK-START: YOUR FIRST 3 PROMPTS IN
10 MINUTES
Get your first results immediately. These three prompts produce useful output with minimal setup.
Prompt 1.1 — Multi-VLAN Network Design
2 minutes of setup, useful output in under a minute
Fill in your environment names (offices, cameras, VoIP, guest Wi-Fi), an approximate device count
per segment, and the gear you have on hand. You'll get a VLAN table, trunking
recommendations, and inter-VLAN routing rules you can start working with immediately.
Prompt 1.3 — Critical Topology Review
Best for: you just inherited a network and need a structured starting point
Describe your current setup in plain text — devices, connections, VLANs, IP ranges, services. The
AI will give you a prioritized list of weaknesses (P1/P2/P3) and a recommended remediation
sequence. This is a structured thinking tool — it will surface issues for you to evaluate, not a
substitute for a professional audit.
Prompt 5.3 — Auto-Generate Network Documentation (see STAGE3_EN_Cat3-5.md)
Best for: infrastructure that exists but has never been properly documented
Paste your existing configuration or describe your infrastructure, and get a structured technical
documentation template populated with your data. Useful for creating the first draft of
documentation for a client or employer.

CATEGORY 1 — NETWORK TOPOLOGY
DESIGN
6 prompts covering multi-VLAN design, IP addressing, topology review, IoT/camera/VoIP
segmentation, scalability planning, and Mermaid diagram generation.
1.1 Design a Multi-VLAN Network from Scratch
Difficulty: Intermediate
(Requires: working knowledge of VLANs, subnetting, basic firewall concepts)
When to Use
Use this prompt at the start of a new network deployment or when redesigning an existing flat
network into a properly segmented multi-VLAN architecture. It's particularly effective when you
have a clear list of environments to segment (offices, cameras, VoIP, guest Wi-Fi, IoT,
management) but haven't yet committed to specific VLAN IDs, subnets, or inter-VLAN routing
policies.
It's also useful as a structured starting point for client conversations — run the prompt with their
stated requirements to generate a first-draft design you can review together, then refine.
Not a substitute for: a professional topology review (use prompt 1.3), a full IP addressing plan
(use prompt 1.2), or security policy design (use Category 4 prompts).

Expected Output
A complete VLAN design including:
- VLAN table: ID, name, subnet, gateway, purpose, recommended DHCP range
- Inter-switch trunking recommendations
- Inter-VLAN routing policy (what communicates with what, and how)
- Key security controls to implement immediately

[EQUIPMENT: list your hardware, e.g., Fortinet FortiGate 60F (firewall/gateway), D-Link DG
SPECIAL REQUIREMENTS:
[SPECIAL-REQUIREMENTS: e.g., PoE for cameras, QoS priority for VoIP, complete isolation fo
Design for me:
1. A VLAN table with: VLAN ID, name, subnet (/24 recommended), gateway, purpose, recommend
2. Inter-switch trunking recommendations: which ports, which VLANs tagged, which VLAN is n
3. Inter-VLAN routing policy: which VLANs can communicate with which, via what mechanism (
4. Key security controls to implement before going live (top 5, prioritized)
Format: structured table for item 1, numbered bullet points for items 2–4. Be specific and

Fortinet FortiGate 60F (firewall/gateway/DHCP/NAT), D-Link DGS-1210-52TP (PoE switch, 52 p

PoE required for cameras (ports 5–12) and APs. QoS/CoS priority for VoIP.
Complete isolation for guest Wi-Fi (internet-only, no access to internal VLANs).

2. Trunking Recommendations
fw-edge-01 (LAN port) →  sw-core-01 (port 52): trunk, tag VLANs 10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70.
Native/untagged: VLAN 60 (management). This carries all inter-VLAN routing traffic through
the firewall.
sw-core-01 (port 51) →  sw-core-02 (port 1): trunk, tag VLANs 20, 30, 40, 60. (Camera and
IoT ports may be on sw-core-01 PoE only — scope this trunk to VLANs actually needed on sw-
core-02.)
sw-core-01 (dedicated port) →  wlc-01: trunk, tag VLANs 10 and 70. The WLC maps Corp-
WiFi SSID →  VLAN 10, Guest SSID →  VLAN 70.
PoE access ports for cameras: sw-core-01 ports 5–12, access (untagged) VLAN 20. PoE
enabled.

4. Key Security Controls (prioritized)
1. Enable client isolation on the Guest Wi-Fi SSID — prevents guest devices from scanning or
communicating with each other and catching other VLAN traffic. Test by connecting two
devices as guests and attempting a ping between them.
2. Restrict VLAN 60 access to known admin IP addresses — Management VLAN firewall policy
source: admin-host group only. Block all access from all other VLANs.
3. Disable internet access for cameras (VLAN 20) — create an explicit DENY rule from VLAN
20 to WAN before the default ALLOW rule. Cameras with cloud features will stop working; this
is intentional.
4. Change default credentials on every device — FortiGate factory admin, D-Link switch web UI
admin, WLC admin, NVR admin, all IP cameras. Do this before connecting any device to the
production VLAN.
5. Enable port security or DHCP snooping on access ports — prevents rogue DHCP servers
and unauthorized devices. [VERIFY: D-Link DGS-1210-52TP DHCP Snooping support in CLI
Reference Guide]
Variations and Follow-Ups
After receiving the initial design, refine it with these follow-up prompts:

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 16 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
"Rewrite item 3 (inter-VLAN routing policy) as Fortinet FortiGate 60F firewall policy CLI
commands (FortiOS 7.4 syntax)."
"Add a DMZ segment for a self-hosted web application that needs selective access from
VLAN 40 and internet inbound on port 443."
"Redesign this for a Cisco IOS-XE core switch instead of D-Link, keeping the same VLAN IDs
and subnets."
"Generate a risk assessment for this design — what are the top 3 single points of failure and
how would you mitigate each?"
"Adapt this design for a multi-site deployment: two physical locations connected by an IKEv2
IPsec VPN tunnel, same VLAN scheme but non-overlapping subnets."
Warning / Safety Note
⚠  The VLAN and subnet design produced by this prompt is a starting proposal, not a finished
configuration. Verify there are no IP conflicts with your existing infrastructure before implementing.
Inter-VLAN routing policies must be reviewed and tested before going live — an incorrect DENY rule
can lock you out of your own equipment, and an incorrect ALLOW rule can expose sensitive
segments. Always test on a lab or non-production switch first. If you're taking over an existing
network, run prompt 1.3 (Critical Topology Review) before making changes.
1.2 Structured IP Addressing and Subnetting Plan
Difficulty: Beginner–Intermediate
(Requires: basic subnetting knowledge; /24 fixed-scheme output is accessible to juniors)
When to Use
Use this prompt when you need a clean, documented IP addressing scheme before or during a
network deployment. It's ideal for:
- Starting a new site from scratch and needing a structured plan before configuring DHCP scopes
- Auditing an existing network for addressing conflicts or growth limitations
- Explaining an addressing scheme to a client or non-technical manager (the output formats well
as a presentation table)
Use this before prompt 2.3 (FortiGate DHCP Server configuration) and before prompt 2.5 (Switch
Bootstrap), since both require confirmed IP assignments.

Scalability notes: All /24 segments provide 254 usable addresses — more than adequate for all
stated host counts with 3–4x growth room. Corp-WiFi (80 hosts, 100 addresses reserved) has the
tightest margin; if density exceeds ~180 concurrent clients, consider a second SSID mapped to a
new VLAN (e.g., VLAN 11 →  10.10.11.0/24). Management VLAN is static-only — document every
static assignment before deployment.

address list (FortiOS) on existing gear to confirm no device currently holds any address in
10.10.10–70.x. Check any site-to-site VPN pools for overlap. Verify your ISP's modem/gateway
does not use 10.10.x.x internally.

Warning / Safety Note
⚠  Verify there are no conflicts with your existing infrastructure before assigning these
addresses. AI has no knowledge of your current network state. A /24 that "looks free" to the AI may
already be in use on a VPN tunnel, a cloud VPC peering connection, or a legacy server. Also verify
with your internet provider that their CPE does not use addresses in your chosen range — some
SMB routers use 10.10.x.x by default. When in doubt, use 192.168.x.0/24 ranges, which are less likely
to conflict with ISP gear.

1.3 Critical Review of an Existing Network Topology
Difficulty: Advanced
(Requires: ability to describe and interpret network architecture; critical thinking about what the
AI surfaces is essential)
When to Use
Use this prompt when you inherit a network — from a new client, a job change, or a post-merger
integration — and need a structured first assessment. It's also effective for periodic self-audits of
your own infrastructure.
This prompt is a structured thinking tool, not an automated audit. The quality of what it surfaces
depends entirely on the quality of your infrastructure description. The more specific your input,
the more actionable the output.
Typical use cases:
- Pre-project assessment for a new client engagement
- Annual architecture review
- Pre-migration check before a significant infrastructure change
- Training juniors to identify common design weaknesses
Required Inputs
Placeholder What to provide Example
[TOPOLOGY-
DESCRIPTION]
Full text description of
the network
List all devices, how they connect, VLAN IDs, IP
ranges, firewall type, access methods, critical
services
The more detail you provide, the more specific the analysis. Include: device models, firmware
versions, VLAN scheme, IP ranges, upstream connectivity, remote access method, redundancy
(or lack of it), known special requirements (VoIP, cameras, IoT), and any known issues.
Expected Output
A structured analysis with: strengths, Priority 1 / Priority 2 / Priority 3 issues with specific impact
and remediation guidance, and a top-5 action plan in recommended execution order.

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 20 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
The Prompt
You are a senior network consultant hired to perform a critical infrastructure review.
CURRENT TOPOLOGY:
[TOPOLOGY-DESCRIPTION: describe the network in as much technical detail as possible, inclu
- All devices: make, model, approximate firmware/OS version
- How devices are connected: physical topology, port numbers if known
- VLAN scheme: IDs, names, subnets, what's on each VLAN
- IP addressing: ranges used, gateway addresses, DHCP server location
- Firewall/gateway: make, model, how it's configured (NAT, inter-VLAN routing method)
- Remote access: how admins access the network remotely (VPN type, SSH, RDP, nothing)
- Critical services: which systems must not go down, and their current resilience
- Wireless infrastructure: SSID count, authentication method, VLAN mapping
- Known issues or concerns: anything you already know is suboptimal
- Age of equipment: approximate]
Analyze this topology and provide:
1. STRENGTHS: What is well-designed and should be preserved
2. P1 ISSUES (immediate action required): For each — specific problem, exact risk or conse
3. P2 ISSUES (address within 30–90 days): For each — specific problem, potential impact, r
4. P3 IMPROVEMENTS (non-urgent optimizations): For each — what to improve, why, and rough 
5. ACTION PLAN: The top 5 interventions in recommended execution order, with rationale for
Be specific and technical. Avoid generic statements like "improve security." Name the exac
Worked Example
Sample Input:
CURRENT TOPOLOGY:
Devices: Fortinet FortiGate 60F (FortiOS 7.2) as gateway/firewall/DHCP, 
  D-Link DGS-1210-52TP (sw-core-01, PoE) as core switch,
  D-Link DGS-1210-52 (sw-core-02) as secondary switch connected to sw-core-01 via single u
  D-Link DNH-1000 WLC + 2x DAP-X3060 APs.
Connections: FortiGate LAN port →  sw-core-01 port 48 (single uplink, no redundancy).
  sw-core-01 port 1 →  sw-core-02 port 1 (single cable, 1Gbps).
VLAN scheme: VLAN 10 (Corp-WiFi), VLAN 20 (Cameras), VLAN 30 (VoIP), 
  VLAN 40 (Internal offices), VLAN 50 (IoT), VLAN 60 (Management).
  No guest VLAN. Guest users go on Corp-WiFi SSID with no isolation.

  Management: 192.168.11.0/24 — no source IP restriction on management access.

  SSH to switches available on any VLAN.

  WPA2-PSK on all SSIDs including corporate.

Sample Output (illustrative):
STRENGTHS:
- FortiGate as a centralized DHCP + firewall is sound SMB architecture — maintains single policy
point

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 21 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
- VLAN segmentation is present and covers the main risk categories (cameras, IoT, VoIP isolated)
- Equipment is 2 years old — still within typical support window [VERIFY current FortiGate 60F
EOS dates at fortiguard.com]
P1 ISSUES (immediate):
1. Guest Wi-Fi shares VLAN 10 with corporate employees. Impact: Any guest device can scan,
communicate with, or attack every wired and wireless corporate device. Guest ARP cache
poisoning attacks are trivially executable in this configuration. Remediation: Create VLAN 70
(Guest-WiFi, separate subnet), map Guest SSID to VLAN 70, enable client isolation on that
SSID, add a DENY rule from VLAN 70 to all internal VLANs.
2. FortiGate HTTPS management exposed on WAN port 443, no MFA. Impact: Credential
stuffing attacks are continuous against exposed Fortinet management interfaces. Without
MFA, a single stolen or guessed password grants full administrative access to the firewall and
all policies. Remediation: Disable admin access on the WAN interface immediately. Enable SSL-
VPN for remote administration. Enable FortiToken or TOTP-based MFA for all admin accounts.
3. Management VLAN (VLAN 60) accessible from any VLAN without source IP restriction.
Impact: Any compromised device on any VLAN can attempt to log into switches and the
firewall. Remediation: Create an admin-host group with specific allowed admin IPs; apply it as
the source filter on all firewall policies permitting access to VLAN 60.
P2 ISSUES (30–90 days):
1. Single uplink from FortiGate to sw-core-01 — single point of failure for the entire network.
Consider link aggregation (LACP) if the FortiGate 60F supports it [VERIFY: check Fortinet 60F
datasheet for LACP/LAG support on LAN ports], or a redundant uplink path.
2. WPA2-PSK on corporate SSID — PSK is shared; any departing employee retains wireless
access until the passphrase is changed (which affects all devices). Migrate to WPA3-Enterprise
with a RADIUS server or at minimum WPA3-Personal with individual certificates.
P3 IMPROVEMENTS:
1. Migrate odd-subnet IP scheme to clean 10.10.x.0/24 addressing to reduce operational
confusion.
2. Enable Syslog forwarding from all devices to a centralized syslog server.
3. Document VLAN-to-port assignments in a network inventory sheet.
TOP 5 ACTION PLAN:
1. Move Guest SSID to isolated VLAN 70 (safety: prevents guest–corp lateral movement)
2. Disable FortiGate WAN management access; enable VPN + MFA (safety: closes the highest-
priority remote attack surface)
3. Restrict Management VLAN access to admin IPs (containment: limits blast radius if any device
is compromised)
4. Implement WPA3-Personal or WPA3-Enterprise on Corp-WiFi SSID (authentication: closes
wireless credential risk)
5. Document and verify all VLAN-to-port assignments (operational: eliminates the "occasional
management drop" by finding and fixing the misconfigured trunk port)
Variations and Follow-Ups
"Focus specifically on the firewall policy review. Generate a checklist of FortiOS firewall policy
checks I should perform manually on this device."
"Convert P1 issue #1 into a step-by-step remediation procedure I can hand to a junior
engineer."

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 22 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
"What would a SOC analyst see in the logs if Guest Wi-Fi users were already performing ARP
spoofing on VLAN 10? What log entries should I search for?"
"Estimate the effort (hours) to remediate all P1 issues for a one-person IT team."
Warning / Safety Note
⚠  The AI's analysis is based solely on the description you provided. Issues it identifies may
already be mitigated by configurations you didn't mention. Issues it doesn't identify may be real
problems in your infrastructure that weren't visible from your description. Use the output as a
structured starting checklist, not as the final word on your network security posture. Before acting
on any P1 finding, verify the finding directly on the device — don't apply a "fix" to a problem the AI
assumed exists without confirming it actually does.
1.4 Segmentation for IoT, Surveillance, and VoIP
Difficulty: Intermediate–Advanced
(Requires: VLAN knowledge, firewall policy concepts, understanding of IoT security risks)
When to Use
Use this prompt when designing secure isolation for high-risk device categories: IP cameras,
NVRs, IoT sensors, smart home/building devices, and VoIP phones + PBX. These device
categories have three things in common: they're often poorly secured by the manufacturer,
they're always-on, and compromising them can give an attacker persistent network access or a
surveillance capability.
This prompt designs the isolation architecture — it works alongside prompt 1.1 (full VLAN design)
but goes deeper on security rules, firmware update policies, and device-specific risks.

4. Device-Specific Risks and Mitigations
Cameras: Default credentials (admin/12345 on Hikvision factory default) — change immediately
before connecting to VLAN 20. Hikvision devices have had multiple CVEs including
unauthenticated RCE ([VERIFY: check Hikvision PSIRT at hikvision.com/en/about/PSIRT/ for
current advisories]). Disable all cloud features (Hik-Connect) unless required; they create
outbound connections to Hikvision cloud infrastructure. Enable HTTPS-only access to NVR web
interface.
IoT: Smart plugs with local API (Shelly) are better than cloud-only devices for security. The hub is
the highest-value target — keep Home Assistant updated and restrict admin access to VLAN 40
only. Disable Telnet on all IoT devices; use SSH or HTTPS only.
VoIP: SIP is inherently vulnerable to toll fraud — an exposed SIP port allows attackers to make
calls billed to your account. Ensure SIP trunk uses TLS (5061) and SRTP where the provider
supports it [VERIFY: check your SIP trunk provider's documentation]. Restrict PBX inbound SIP to
known trunk provider IP ranges.
5. Common Mistakes
Putting NVR on a VLAN that can reach the internet "for cloud backup" — this exposes your
surveillance system to remote attack and bypasses isolation entirely
Allowing the IoT hub to reach ALL internet rather than only specific update servers — limit
outbound from the hub to known FQDNs if your firewall supports FQDN filtering

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 25 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
Forgetting to configure client isolation on IoT Wi-Fi SSID — IoT devices can scan and attack
each other if on the same wireless segment without isolation
Using the same WPA2 passphrase for IoT-Net and Corp-WiFi — always use different
passphrases; if an IoT device is compromised, the attacker shouldn't also have your corporate
Wi-Fi password
Variations and Follow-Ups
"Write the FortiGate 60F CLI commands for all firewall policies listed in section 2 above
(FortiOS 7.4 syntax)."
"What Zeek/Suricata detection rules would flag anomalous behavior from a camera on VLAN
20 attempting to reach other VLANs?"
"Generate a security onboarding checklist for every new IoT device added to this network."
"Adapt the VoIP design for a cloud PBX (RingCentral / 3CX Cloud) instead of self-hosted
Asterisk — what changes?"
Warning / Safety Note
⚠  Critical: IP cameras and IoT devices are historically the most exploited device categories in SMB
networks (Mirai botnet and variants target default credentials on these device types at scale).
Isolation in a dedicated VLAN with no internet access is mandatory, not optional — it is the primary
mitigation. The firewall rules above must be tested explicitly: connect a camera and verify it cannot
reach a PC on VLAN 40 and cannot ping 8.8.8.8. Do not assume the rules work because you
configured them. Always change default credentials before connecting any device to the network.
1.5 Network Scalability Roadmap (2–3 Years)
Difficulty: Intermediate

When to Use
Use this prompt when a client or manager asks "what does the next 2–3 years of infrastructure
investment look like?" It's particularly useful before budget planning cycles, when onboarding a
new client for long-term infrastructure management, or when significant growth (new office, major
headcount increase, new services) is anticipated.
This prompt produces a phased roadmap, not an implementation plan — it's a strategic planning
tool that gives you a structured starting point for conversations about priorities and budget.

⚠  Always verify the generated Mermaid code at mermaid.live before including it in official
documentation. AI-generated Mermaid for complex topologies (>15 nodes) regularly fails with
syntax errors. Specific known failure modes: node IDs containing hyphens or spaces (use
alphanumeric IDs and put display text in quotes), pipe characters in labels (must be escaped or
avoided), and deeply nested subgraphs. Both graph TD and flowchart TD are valid in Mermaid
v10+ — prefer flowchart TD as the modern syntax. If the generated code fails, ask the AI: "The
code produces a parse error at line X — fix only that line."

2.1 Configure a Trunk Link Between Managed Switches
Difficulty: Intermediate
(Requires: understanding of 802.1Q tagging, VLAN basics, switch CLI access)
When to Use
Use this prompt when connecting two managed switches and needing to carry multiple VLANs
across the link (a trunk). This is required any time a new switch is added to an existing multi-
VLAN network, or when adding a WLC that needs to carry multiple wireless VLANs.
Works for D-Link DGS-1210 series (primary), with adaptation notes for Cisco IOS-XE and Aruba
CX. The AI adapts the output to your specified switch model.
Important: Have console (serial) access to both switches before beginning this configuration. A
trunk misconfiguration can disconnect your management session immediately.
Required Inputs
Placeholder What to provide Example
[SWITCH-A-
MODEL]
Switch A make, model,
firmware
D-Link DGS-1210-52TP (sw-core-01), firmware
6.10
[SWITCH-A-PORT] Switch A trunk port Port 48
[SWITCH-B-
MODEL]
Switch B make, model,
firmware
D-Link DGS-1210-52 (sw-core-02), firmware
6.10
[SWITCH-B-PORT] Switch B trunk port Port 1
[VLANS-TO-
TRUNK]
VLAN IDs to carry 10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70
[NATIVE-VLAN] Native/untagged VLAN VLAN 60 (Management)
Expected Output
Complete CLI commands for both switches (create VLANs if not present, configure trunk ports
with all VLANs tagged, set native VLAN), verification commands, and common error diagnosis
guidance.

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition

Configure an 802.1Q trunk link between two switches.

  Model: [SWITCH-A-MODEL]
  Trunk port: [SWITCH-A-PORT]
  Current VLANs already configured: [VLANS-EXISTING-ON-A or "none"]
SWITCH B:
  Model: [SWITCH-B-MODEL]
  Trunk port: [SWITCH-B-PORT]
  Current VLANs already configured: [VLANS-EXISTING-ON-B or "none"]
VLANs to carry on the trunk: [VLANS-TO-TRUNK]
Native / untagged VLAN: [NATIVE-VLAN]
Generate:
1. CLI commands for Switch A: create any missing VLANs, configure trunk port with all VLAN
2. CLI commands for Switch B: same
3. Verification commands for both switches: confirm trunk is up, confirm each VLAN is carr
4. A brief explanation of what each command block does and why
5. The top 3 configuration mistakes in this setup and how to avoid them
Important: If D-Link DGS-1210 series, note that CLI access requires SSH or console. Specif
Worked Example
Sample Input:
SWITCH A: D-Link DGS-1210-52TP (sw-core-01), trunk port 48, VLANs 10/20/30/40/50/60/70 not
SWITCH B: D-Link DGS-1210-52 (sw-core-02), trunk port 1, no VLANs configured
VLANS TO TRUNK: 10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70
NATIVE VLAN: 60 (Management)

Link DGS-1210-52TP CLI Reference Guide]):

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 34 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
# ============================================================
# SWITCH A — sw-core-01 (D-Link DGS-1210-52TP)
# Execute from console or SSH session
# ============================================================
# Step 1: Create VLANs with names
create vlan Corp-WiFi tag 10
create vlan Cameras tag 20
create vlan VoIP tag 30
create vlan Internal tag 40
create vlan IoT tag 50
create vlan Management tag 60
create vlan Guest-WiFi tag 70
# Step 2: Configure port 48 as trunk — tag all VLANs
config 802.1q vlan 10 add tagged 48
config 802.1q vlan 20 add tagged 48
config 802.1q vlan 30 add tagged 48
config 802.1q vlan 40 add tagged 48
config 802.1q vlan 50 add tagged 48
config 802.1q vlan 60 add tagged 48
config 802.1q vlan 70 add tagged 48
# Step 3: Set native (untagged/PVID) VLAN on port 48
config 802.1q vlan 60 add untagged 48
config 802.1q vlan default delete 48
config vlan_translation ports 48 pvid 60
# Step 4: Save configuration
save
# ============================================================
# SWITCH B — sw-core-02 (D-Link DGS-1210-52)
# ============================================================
create vlan Corp-WiFi tag 10
create vlan Cameras tag 20
create vlan VoIP tag 30
create vlan Internal tag 40
create vlan IoT tag 50
create vlan Management tag 60
create vlan Guest-WiFi tag 70
config 802.1q vlan 10 add tagged 1
config 802.1q vlan 20 add tagged 1
config 802.1q vlan 30 add tagged 1
config 802.1q vlan 40 add tagged 1
config 802.1q vlan 50 add tagged 1
config 802.1q vlan 60 add tagged 1
config 802.1q vlan 70 add tagged 1
config 802.1q vlan 60 add untagged 1
config 802.1q vlan default delete 1
config vlan_translation ports 1 pvid 60
save
# ============================================================
# VERIFICATION — run on each switch after configuration
# ============================================================
show 802.1q vlan
# Expected: all VLANs 10/20/30/40/50/60/70 listed with tagged ports
show 802.1q vlan id 60
# Expected: port 48 (sw-core-01) or port 1 (sw-core-02) shows as both tagged AND untagged 
show vlan
# Shows VLAN membership summary

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 35 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
# Cross-verify: from a device on VLAN 60 (management), ping both switch management IPs
ping 10.10.60.10
ping 10.10.60.11
[VERIFY] D-Link DGS-1210-52TP CLI syntax varies between firmware versions 6.x and newer. The
config vlan_translation ports X pvid Y command for setting PVID may differ. Verify against
your firmware's CLI Reference Guide at dlink.com/support/productinfo.aspx. Web GUI alternative:
Switching →  802.1Q VLAN →  Edit each VLAN, set port as Tagged for trunk ports.
Top 3 mistakes:
1. Native VLAN mismatch — if Switch A native is VLAN 60 and Switch B native is VLAN 1, traffic
will appear on the wrong VLAN with no error message. Always verify native VLAN is identical on
both ends after configuration.
2. Forgetting to create VLANs before adding them to the trunk — on D-Link, you must create
the VLAN first (create vlan name tag id), then add ports to it. Adding a port to a non-existent
VLAN silently fails.
3. Losing management access during configuration — if your current SSH session is on VLAN
60, and you modify the port carrying VLAN 60 without saving correctly, you may lose access.
Always have physical console access as a backup.
Variations and Follow-Ups
"Translate these commands to Cisco IOS-XE syntax for a Catalyst 9300 trunk port."
"Add VLAN 80 (Server DMZ) to an existing trunk between sw-core-01 and sw-core-02
without disrupting current traffic."
"Generate a pre- and post-change verification checklist for this trunk configuration as a
runbook table."
"What would the STP topology look like with two switches in this trunk configuration? Is there a
loop risk?"
Warning / Safety Note
⚠  Perform this configuration with physical console access to both switches. A native VLAN
mismatch between the two ends of a trunk can cause STP loops or silent traffic misclassification
with no error message. A misconfigured trunk port carrying the management VLAN can immediately
terminate your SSH/Telnet management session. Before starting: verify which port your current
session is coming in on, and confirm it will still be accessible after the trunk configuration. Test each
VLAN after configuration — connect a device to a VLAN 20 access port and verify it receives an IP
in 10.10.20.0/24, not 10.10.60.0/24.

step that makes a port "belong to" a VLAN.

2.5). This prompt handles access ports only — trunk ports are covered in 2.1.

# ============================================================
# sw-core-01: VLAN and Access Port Assignment
# VLANs 10 and 70 are trunk-only (already on trunk port 48)
# ============================================================
# Create VLANs (skip if already created in step 2.1)
create vlan Corp-WiFi tag 10
create vlan Cameras tag 20
create vlan VoIP tag 30
create vlan Internal tag 40
create vlan IoT tag 50
create vlan Management tag 60
create vlan Guest-WiFi tag 70
# Assign access ports — VLAN 20 (Cameras), ports 5-12
config 802.1q vlan 20 add untagged 5-12
config 802.1q vlan default delete 5-12
config vlan_translation ports 5-12 pvid 20
# Assign access ports — VLAN 30 (VoIP), ports 13-20
config 802.1q vlan 30 add untagged 13-20
config 802.1q vlan default delete 13-20
config vlan_translation ports 13-20 pvid 30
# Assign access ports — VLAN 40 (Internal), ports 21-40
config 802.1q vlan 40 add untagged 21-40
config 802.1q vlan default delete 21-40
config vlan_translation ports 21-40 pvid 40
# Assign access ports — VLAN 50 (IoT), ports 41-44
config 802.1q vlan 50 add untagged 41-44
config 802.1q vlan default delete 41-44
config vlan_translation ports 41-44 pvid 50
# Assign access ports — VLAN 60 (Management), ports 45-47
config 802.1q vlan 60 add untagged 45-47
config 802.1q vlan default delete 45-47
config vlan_translation ports 45-47 pvid 60
# Enable PoE on camera ports (5-12) and AP ports (49-50)
config poe ports 5-12 state enable
config poe ports 49-50 state enable
# Save configuration
save
# ============================================================
# VERIFICATION
# ============================================================
show 802.1q vlan
# Check each VLAN shows correct tagged and untagged ports
show poe ports 5-12
# Verify PoE is enabled and devices are drawing power
show vlan
# Summary view of all VLANs and their port members

Warning / Safety Note
⚠  The management port (port 48, trunk) must not be modified during this procedure. If you
accidentally assign port 48 to a VLAN as an access port, you will lose management connectivity
immediately. Perform this configuration from a physical console port if possible. After completing
VLAN assignments, verify by plugging a test device into each access port and confirming it receives
a DHCP address in the correct subnet (e.g., port in VLAN 20 should get 10.10.20.x from DHCP). An
incorrect PVID assignment means the device gets an IP from the wrong VLAN — this is silent and
only shows during testing.
2.3 Configure Fortinet FortiGate as Multi-VLAN DHCP Server
Difficulty: Intermediate–Advanced
(Requires: FortiOS CLI access, understanding of VLAN interfaces, IP plan from prompt 1.2)
When to Use
Use this prompt when FortiGate is acting as the centralized gateway and DHCP server for all
VLANs — the most common SMB configuration with a single FortiGate appliance. FortiGate
creates one sub-interface per VLAN on a physical LAN port, and each sub-interface runs its own
DHCP server pool.
Run this after prompts 1.1 (VLAN design) and 1.2 (IP addressing plan). Have the IP plan from
prompt 1.2 ready before running this — you'll be filling in specific subnet, gateway, and range
values.
FortiOS version note: All CLI examples below target FortiOS 7.4. Verify syntax against your firmware
version at docs.fortinet.com. [VERIFY: FortiGate 60F DHCP server CLI syntax in FortiOS 7.4
Administration Guide.]

DHCP and Firewall Policy Note: FortiGate's built-in DHCP server on each VLAN interface
automatically responds to DHCP Discover/Request from that interface — no explicit firewall policy is
needed for DHCP itself. However, inter-VLAN communication (e.g., VLAN 40 workstations reaching
VLAN 40 servers) requires an explicit firewall policy from the source interface to the destination
interface. Without a matching policy, traffic between VLANs is silently dropped even though devices
have valid IPs. Use prompt 4.1 (Security Policy Review) to generate baseline inter-VLAN policies.
Variations and Follow-Ups
"Generate the firewall policies for the inter-VLAN routing rules from prompt 1.1 — source
interface to destination interface with explicit DENY and ALLOW rules."
"How do I configure DHCP relay on FortiGate if I move to an external DHCP server (e.g.,
Windows Server DHCP) for VLAN 40 only?"
"Add a DHCP option 43 (vendor-specific option for Cisco IP phones) to the VLAN 30 DHCP
server config."

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 43 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
"Configure FortiGate to assign different DNS servers to VLAN 70 (Guest) — use 1.1.1.1 and
1.0.0.1 instead of 8.8.8.8."
Warning / Safety Note
⚠  On FortiGate, each VLAN interface requires an explicit firewall policy for traffic to flow. A client
in VLAN 40 with a valid IP and gateway will still have zero inter-VLAN connectivity if no firewall
policy exists — this is a common point of confusion. DHCP Discover/Offer works even without a
policy (it's handled at the interface level), but all other traffic requires a policy. Also: do not set
allowaccess https ssh on VLAN 10, 20, 30, 50, or 70 interfaces — this would expose FortiGate
management to untrusted segments. Only VLAN 60 (Management) should have admin access
enabled on its interface.

Warning / Safety Note
⚠  Test guest client isolation explicitly before declaring the wireless configuration complete.
Connect two devices to the Guest SSID and attempt a ping between them — many WLC
implementations have bugs where client isolation is configured but not enforced correctly. Also test
that a guest device cannot reach 10.10.40.1 (or any internal VLAN IP) — if it can, the firewall policy
for VLAN 70 is missing or misconfigured. Do not trust GUI checkboxes without a live verification
test.

⚠  Apply this script via console (physical serial or USB-to-serial) connection only. If you apply via

via console anyway. Setting the management IP in the wrong VLAN or with a wrong netmask can
make the device unreachable — factory reset is then required. Do NOT include the admin password
in this script or in the AI prompt — the credential must be applied manually after the script runs to
avoid it appearing in AI service logs or prompt history.
2.6 Configure QoS for VoIP Traffic
Difficulty: Advanced
(Requires: understanding of DSCP, 802.1p CoS, traffic shaping; impacts all devices on the path)
When to Use
Use this prompt when VoIP call quality is degraded — choppy audio, dropped syllables, echo, or
high jitter — or proactively, before deploying VoIP in an environment where QoS has not been
configured. VoIP is highly sensitive to jitter and delay; without QoS, a large file transfer on the
same segment can degrade call quality for all simultaneous calls.
Important: QoS is only effective end-to-end. Configuring one hop is not sufficient — every device
between the IP phone and the PBX (and between the PBX and the PSTN/SIP trunk) must have
QoS enabled for it to have effect.
Required Inputs
Placeholder What to provide Example
[DEVICES] All devices in the VoIP path D-Link DGS-1210-52TP (switch), Fortinet FortiGate
60F
[VOIP-
VLAN]
VoIP VLAN ID and subnet VLAN 30, 10.10.30.0/24
[CODEC] Voice codec in use G.711 (~85 Kbps/call) or G.729 (~24 Kbps/call)
[MAX-
CALLS]
Maximum simultaneous
calls
8
[UPLINK-
BW]
WAN uplink bandwidth 200 Mbps symmetric fiber

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 50 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
Expected Output

shaping for VoIP VLAN, QoS verification tests with target metrics, and troubleshooting guidance.

MAXIMUM SIMULTANEOUS CALLS: [MAX-CALLS]

Configure:
1. Traffic classification: mark VoIP bearer traffic as DSCP EF (DSCP 46, per RFC 3246) and
2. Priority queues on the switch: strict priority queue for voice (CoS 5 →  DSCP EF); expla
3. Traffic shaping or policing on the firewall for VLAN 30 — calculate the bandwidth reser
4. QoS verification: how to test that QoS is working. Include tools and target metrics:
   - One-way latency: ≤  150ms (per ITU-T G.114 recommendation) [cite: ITU-T G.114]
   - Jitter: ≤  30ms (practical working target; see RFC 3550 for RTP jitter measurement)
   - Packet loss: < 1% for acceptable MOS; < 0.1% for transparent quality
5. Troubleshooting: the top 5 reasons VoIP quality is poor even after QoS configuration, a
Note: DSCP EF marking should be trusted from devices in VLAN 30 (trusted segment) and re-m
Worked Example

Sample Output (illustrative — [VERIFY: D-Link DGS-1210-52TP QoS CLI Reference; FortiOS 7.4
Traffic Shaper documentation]):
Bandwidth Calculation:
- G.711: ~85 Kbps per call including RTP headers
- 8 simultaneous calls: 8 × 85 = 680 Kbps bidirectional
- +20% overhead: 816 Kbps ≈  reserve 1 Mbps on the WAN for VoIP
- On a 200 Mbps uplink, this is < 1% — VoIP is not bandwidth-constrained; jitter management is
the priority
Switch QoS (D-Link DGS-1210-52TP):

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 51 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
# Enable QoS
enable traffic_segmentation
# Map CoS 5 to highest priority queue (Queue 7 on DGS-1210)
# [VERIFY: DGS-1210-52TP QoS →  802.1p Default Priority for port 13-20 (VLAN 30 ports)]
# GUI: QoS →  802.1p Priority Settings →  Set ports 13-20 to CoS 5 default
# Set strict priority for highest queue
config bandwidth_control ports 13-20 rx_rate no_limit tx_rate no_limit
# [VERIFY: strict priority queue CLI on DGS-1210-52TP — GUI preferred for QoS configuratio
FortiGate Traffic Shaper (FortiOS 7.4):
config firewall shaper traffic-shaper
    edit "VoIP-VLAN30-Priority"
        set guaranteed-bandwidth 1000
        set maximum-bandwidth 10000
        set priority high
    next
end
config firewall policy
    edit [VOIP-POLICY-ID]
        set srcintf "port2.30"
        set dstintf "wan1"
        set traffic-shaper "VoIP-VLAN30-Priority"
        set traffic-shaper-reverse "VoIP-VLAN30-Priority"
    next
end
QoS Verification:
- Tool: ping -f -l 1400 (Windows) to force large packets through the path while making a call —
call quality should not degrade
- Tool: Wireshark with rtp display filter — check "Delta" column for jitter spikes > 30ms
- Tool: iperf3 between a VLAN 40 PC and the internet — run simultaneous VoIP call — call
quality should not degrade
- Target metrics: one-way latency ≤  150ms (ITU-T G.114), jitter ≤  30ms, packet loss < 1%
Top 5 QoS Failure Reasons:
1. ISP router/modem does not honor QoS markings — on a fiber connection with a dumb ONT this
is usually fine, but an ISP-managed router may strip DSCP markings. Verify with a packet capture
on WAN side.
2. VoIP traffic is not marked by the phone — check IP phone DSCP settings (admin GUI on the
phone)
3. A single switch on the path has no QoS enabled — QoS is only as strong as the weakest link
4. All VoIP traffic is on a flat network with no QoS — adding QoS after the fact on some but not all
devices has no effect
5. SIP signaling and RTP are being treated identically — SIP (CoS 3 / DSCP CS3) and RTP (CoS 5
/ DSCP EF) should be in different priority classes for correct scheduling
Variations and Follow-Ups
"Calculate the bandwidth needed for G.729 instead of G.711 at 8 simultaneous calls — what
changes in the traffic shaper config?"
"Configure DSCP remarking on FortiGate 60F to force all traffic from VLAN 30 to be marked
DSCP EF regardless of what the phone marks it."

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 52 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
"Generate a SIP ALG analysis for FortiGate — should it be enabled or disabled for this setup,
and what are the symptoms if it's causing issues?"
Warning / Safety Note
⚠  QoS configuration affects all traffic on the configured devices. Misconfigured traffic shaping
can inadvertently throttle non-VoIP traffic below usable levels. Always test under realistic load — run
a file transfer, a video stream, and a VoIP call simultaneously — before declaring QoS working. On
the WAN side, verify with your ISP whether they apply any QoS markings or traffic shaping to your
circuit that could interact with your configuration. QoS settings on a switch require a switch restart
on some D-Link firmware versions — verify this before applying during business hours. [VERIFY:
DGS-1210-52TP QoS restart requirement in firmware release notes]

        set save-password disable
    next

    set servercert "Fortinet_Factory"

- Review VPN access log monthly — flag any successful logins outside of expected

Warning / Safety Note
⚠  Critical — disable direct WAN interface admin access before or immediately after enabling the
VPN. Exposing the FortiGate management interface (HTTPS on port 443 or 8443, SSH on port 22)
directly to the internet means it will be discovered and brute-forced within hours. This is not a
theoretical risk — it is an active, continuous threat. The SSL-VPN itself should use a non-standard
port (the example above uses 8443) and should have MFA enforced — FortiGate allows you to
enforce MFA at the VPN policy level so that users without FortiToken cannot connect even with a
valid password. Log all VPN sessions with a minimum 90-day retention period. [VERIFY: current
Fortinet PSIRT advisories for FortiOS SSL-VPN at fortiguard.com/psirt — FortiOS SSL-VPN has had
high-severity CVEs historically; ensure your firmware is current before enabling this feature.]

CATEGORY 3 — TROUBLESHOOTING
6 prompts covering systematic OSI-layer-by-layer connectivity diagnosis, VLAN trunk faults,
DHCP failures, FortiGate log analysis, pre-go-live checklists, and performance/latency

3.1 Systematic Connectivity Loss Diagnosis (OSI Layer-by-
Layer)
Difficulty: Intermediate
(Requires: OSI model familiarity, basic switch/firewall CLI access, ability to run ipconfig/ip addr
and ping on client devices)
When to Use
Use this prompt whenever a device, VLAN, or segment has lost connectivity and you need a
methodical, layer-by-layer investigation path rather than jumping straight to restarting services or
rebooting hardware. It works best when you can describe the symptom precisely — what works
versus what doesn't, and what you've already tried.
It is particularly effective in two scenarios: (1) a sudden connectivity loss following a recent
change (new device, cable move, firmware update, or configuration edit), and (2) an intermittent
problem where you need to document each layer carefully before the issue disappears again.
The prompt generates a targeted investigation sequence from Layer 1 (physical) through Layer 7
(application), with specific commands for your switch brand and client operating system. It also
produces a decision tree so you can branch based on what each test reveals — instead of
working through every layer sequentially when the issue is already obvious at Layer 2.
Not a substitute for: a full performance analysis (use prompt 3.6 for latency/packet loss), VLAN
trunk–specific troubleshooting (use prompt 3.2), or DHCP-specific issues (use prompt 3.3).

Documentation Checklist
[ ] Record port numbers affected and VLAN before/after change
[ ] Screenshot or paste output of show vlan 40 before and after fix
[ ] Screenshot or paste output of ipconfig /all after successful IP renewal
[ ] Record time of resolution and who made the fix
[ ] Update change log with: date, affected ports, root cause, resolution
Variations and Follow-ups
1. Wireless clients only: If the symptom affects only Wi-Fi clients, skip Layer 1 cable tests and
go straight to confirming SSID →  VLAN mapping on the WLC (wlc-01). Add: "SSID mapping:
Corporate SSID →  VLAN 10, Guest SSID →  VLAN 70, IoT-Net SSID →  VLAN 50."
2. Intermittent connectivity (works sometimes): Add to the symptom: "Occurs 3–4 times per
day, self-resolves in 2–5 minutes." The AI will focus on STP convergence, duplex mismatch,
and uplink saturation as likely causes.
3. Cross-VLAN failure (PC can reach local VLAN, not another VLAN): Layer 2 is fine. Focus the
prompt on inter-VLAN routing policy on fw-edge-01 and firewall policy rules.
4. Follow-up after resolution: "You previously helped me diagnose [symptom]. The issue was
[root cause]. Write a 5-point post-incident summary and identify preventive controls to stop it
recurring."
5. Documentation of troubleshooting session: "Convert the troubleshooting steps above into a
structured technical note suitable for our ticket system and knowledge base."
⚠  Warning / Safety Note
Document every step as you go — before resolving the issue. Troubleshooting notes written after
the fact are unreliable and lose critical detail. Even if you solve the problem in five minutes, a one-
line log entry ("changed PVID on port 15–17 from 10 to 40") is what prevents the same problem
recurring months later.
Do not paste real credentials, passwords, private key material, or proprietary configuration data
into any AI prompt. Use placeholder values for any sensitive fields (credentials, private IPs mapped
to real organizations, NDA-protected topology details).
Do not restart hardware as a first troubleshooting step. Rebooting a core switch or firewall to "see
if it fixes it" erases logs, resets counters, and makes the root cause undiagnosable. Always collect
diagnostic output before any corrective action.
3.2 VLAN Trunk Troubleshooting
Difficulty: Intermediate
(Requires: understanding of 802.1Q trunking, VLAN membership, native VLAN concepts, CLI
access to both switches)

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 63 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
When to Use
Use this prompt when specific VLANs are not passing through an inter-switch trunk link, while
other VLANs on the same trunk work correctly. This pattern — "VLAN X fails, VLANs Y and Z
work" — is almost always a configuration mismatch at the trunk level, not a physical problem.
Common triggers: a new VLAN was added to the infrastructure but was not permitted on all trunk
links; the native VLAN was changed on one switch but not the other; a switch firmware update
reset trunk configuration to defaults; or the trunk was configured manually and one VLAN ID was
accidentally omitted.
The prompt works best when you can provide the show vlan and trunk-configuration output
from both switches. Without these, the AI can only give generic guidance. Paste the raw CLI
output directly — even messy or truncated output is more useful than a text description.

core-02; VLANs 40, 60 work fine"

family of first switch
"D-Link DGS-1210-52TP"
[SWITCH-A-

from Switch A

family of second switch

   to fix the issue on both sides

4. WHY IT HAPPENED and how to prevent recurrence
5. OTHER LATENT ISSUES: any other misconfigurations visible in the trunk output that shoul

Sample Output (illustrative — actual AI output will vary):
1. IDENTIFIED CAUSE
VLAN 20 (Cameras) is tagged on sw-core-01's trunk port 51 but is not defined on sw-core-02 at
all, and is not permitted on sw-core-02's trunk port 1. The trunk link between the two switches
only allows tagged traffic for VLAN 40 from sw-core-02's perspective — all other tagged VLANs
arriving from sw-core-01 are dropped at the ingress of sw-core-02 port 1 because sw-core-02
has no VLAN 20, 10, 30, 50, or 70 in its VLAN database.
This also means VLANs 10, 30, 50, and 70 are similarly broken on sw-core-02 — they just haven't
been noticed yet because those VLANs don't have devices connected to sw-core-02 ports.
Root cause: When sw-core-02 was initially configured, only VLANs 40 and 60 were added to its
VLAN database. The trunk port was configured to carry only VLAN 40 (tagged). All other VLANs
were never provisioned on this switch.
2. CORRECTION COMMANDS (D-Link DGS-1210-52, sw-core-02)

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 66 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
# Step 1 — Create all missing VLANs on sw-core-02
create vlan vlanid 10 name Corp-WiFi
create vlan vlanid 20 name Cameras
create vlan vlanid 30 name VoIP
create vlan vlanid 50 name IoT
create vlan vlanid 70 name Guest-WiFi
# Step 2 — Add port 1 (trunk to sw-core-01) as tagged member of all VLANs
config vlan vlanid 10 add tagged 1
config vlan vlanid 20 add tagged 1
config vlan vlanid 30 add tagged 1
config vlan vlanid 50 add tagged 1
config vlan vlanid 70 add tagged 1
# Step 3 — Save configuration
save
No changes needed on sw-core-01 (port 51 already carries all VLANs correctly).
3. POST-FIX VERIFICATION
# On sw-core-02 — confirm VLAN 20 is now active and tagged on port 1
show vlan 20
# Expected: VLAN 20 listed as Active, port 1 shown as Tagged member
show 802.1Q vlan
# Expected: port 1 now shows Tagged: 10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 70; Untagged: 60
# End-to-end test from a device that should traverse the trunk via VLAN 20:
# From management workstation on VLAN 60:
ping 10.10.20.5    # nvr-cam-01 — should now respond
4. WHY IT HAPPENED / PREVENTION
A partial initial configuration left sw-core-02 with only the minimum VLANs needed at the time of
deployment. As the network grew, VLANs were added to sw-core-01 but not propagated to sw-
core-02.
Prevention: Maintain a VLAN configuration checklist that is applied to all switches whenever a
new VLAN is added to the network. Use a network change SOP (see prompt 5.2) that explicitly
includes "propagate VLAN to all trunk-connected switches" as a mandatory step. Consider using
a network management tool or Ansible playbook to push VLAN changes to all switches
simultaneously.
5. OTHER LATENT ISSUES
VLANs 10, 30, 50, 70 are now added to sw-core-02 but have no access ports assigned. This
is fine for now, but document which ports will eventually be used for each VLAN.
sw-core-02 VLAN database shows VLAN 1 Default covering ports 2–52 — all access ports.
Any access port not explicitly assigned to a VLAN will carry VLAN 1 traffic. This is a security
risk (VLAN hopping). Disable all unused ports and explicitly assign all active ports to their
correct VLANs.
Confirm the native/untagged VLAN is VLAN 60 (Mgmt) on both sides of the trunk. Currently it
is on sw-core-01 port 51; confirm sw-core-02 port 1 also has PVID 60.

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 67 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
Variations and Follow-ups
1. Cisco IOS-XE variant: Replace the D-Link CLI with: "Switch brand is Cisco Catalyst 9300
(IOS-XE 17.x). Use show interfaces trunk, show vlan brief, and switchport trunk allowed
vlan add [ID] commands."
2. Native VLAN mismatch: Change the problem to: "Devices are reachable but in the wrong
VLAN; STP shows topology changes." This triggers diagnosis of PVID/native VLAN mismatch.
3. STP blocking the trunk: Add to the problem: "Trunk link shows Link Up on both sides, but no
VLAN traffic passes." This points toward STP BPDU guard or root guard triggering on the
trunk port.
4. More than 2 switches: Extend the prompt to include a third switch (sw-access-floor2)
between sw-core-01 and end devices. Ask the AI to trace which hop in the chain is the fault.
5. Post-fix documentation: "Generate a one-page change note for this VLAN trunk fix, suitable
for a network change log."
⚠  Warning / Safety Note
Native VLAN mismatch is a silent and dangerous misconfiguration. When the native/untagged
VLAN differs between the two ends of a trunk (e.g., VLAN 1 on Switch A, VLAN 60 on Switch B),
frames destined for the native VLAN on one side are silently moved into the wrong VLAN on the
other side. This can cause STP loop topology changes, unexpected inter-VLAN reachability, or
management traffic becoming invisible — without any obvious error message on either switch.
Always verify the native VLAN (PVID) is identical on both ends of every trunk link before going
live. After any trunk configuration change, confirm that all expected VLANs are reachable end-to-
end — not just the one that was broken.
Do not make trunk changes during production hours. A misconfigured trunk can take down all
VLANs simultaneously, not just the one you're working on.
3.3 DHCP Troubleshooting: No IP, APIPA, or Wrong Subnet
Difficulty: Beginner
(Requires: ability to run ipconfig/ip addr on a client, access to the DHCP server or firewall
management console)
When to Use
Use this prompt when clients fail to obtain a DHCP-assigned IP address, receive an APIPA
address (169.254.x.x), or receive an address from the wrong subnet. This is one of the most
common day-to-day networking problems and typically has one of five causes: an exhausted
DHCP pool, a misconfigured port VLAN (so the client is on the wrong segment), a broken DHCP
relay, a fault on the DHCP server itself, or a client-side issue.
This prompt covers all four sides of the problem — client, switch, DHCP server (whether that's a
FortiGate, a Windows Server, or another platform), and DHCP relay — in a single structured guide.
Provide as much output as you can from the affected clients, because the specific failure mode
(APIPA vs. wrong subnet vs. no IP at all after timeout) narrows the diagnosis significantly.
It is especially useful when only some clients on a VLAN are affected (pointing to pool exhaustion
or a specific port issue) rather than all clients (pointing to a server or relay problem).

Variations and Follow-ups
1. Wrong subnet (receives address from different VLAN): Change the symptom to "Gets IP in
10.10.10.x range but should be 10.10.40.x." This indicates the port is in the wrong VLAN or
there is a trunk native VLAN issue. The AI will focus on port PVID and native VLAN checks.
2. All clients on a VLAN affected simultaneously: This points to server failure or DHCP relay
failure, not pool exhaustion. Add: "All 24 clients on VLAN 40 are affected; clients on VLAN 30
and 10 are fine."
3. DHCP on Windows Server 2022 variant: Replace the server platform with "Windows Server
2022" and ask for the Windows PowerShell equivalents: Get-DhcpServerv4Lease, Get-
DhcpServerv4Scope, Get-DhcpServerv4Statistics.
4. ISC DHCP on Linux variant: Use "ISC DHCP on Ubuntu 22.04" and ask for dhcpd.leases log
parsing and journalctl -u isc-dhcp-server log analysis.
5. Prevent recurrence: "Given this was a pool exhaustion issue, recommend a DHCP monitoring
strategy to alert before the pool reaches 80% utilization."
⚠  Warning / Safety Note
DHCP pool exhaustion is deceptively silent. When a FortiGate (or any DHCP server) runs out of
addresses in a pool, it does not generate a prominent error message in the management GUI — it
simply stops answering DHCP Discover broadcasts. Existing clients with active leases continue
working normally, which is why only new connections fail. Always verify the number of active leases
against the pool size before pursuing other diagnoses.
Reducing lease time has a lag effect. If you reduce the lease time from 24 hours to 4 hours to
recover addresses from inactive clients, existing clients won't renew for up to 12 hours (half the old
lease time). The pool may not free up immediately. You may need to manually clear stale leases.
DHCP snooping is a valid security control — but enabling it without marking the correct trusted
port will block all legitimate DHCP traffic on that VLAN. Always mark the port connected to the
DHCP server (or the uplink trunk carrying DHCP traffic) as "trusted" before enabling DHCP snooping
on access ports. [VERIFY: D-Link DGS-1210-52TP DHCP snooping support and trusted port
configuration in your firmware version's CLI reference]

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 72 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
3.4 FortiGate Log Analysis and Interpretation
Difficulty: Intermediate
(Requires: access to FortiGate log output or export; basic understanding of FortiGate traffic log
structure; FortiOS 7.4 — verify syntax for other versions)
When to Use
Use this prompt when you have FortiGate log output — traffic logs, event logs, IPS alerts, or
Application Control entries — and need to extract operationally meaningful findings from raw
data. It is particularly effective after a policy change (to identify legitimate traffic that was
unexpectedly blocked), during or after a security event (to identify anomalies and suspicious
sources), or when users report application slowness or connectivity issues that may have a
firewall policy cause.
The prompt works on raw FortiGate log data pasted directly from the CLI (diagnose log test,
get log traffic list) or exported as CSV from the FortiGate GUI (Log & Report →  Traffic). The
more log data you provide, the more accurate the analysis.
It is also well-suited for a weekly or monthly proactive log review — run it with 7 or 30 days of
traffic log data to surface anomalies before they become incidents.
Note on AI log analysis: AI analysis of network logs is a methodological support tool. It can surface
patterns, anomalies, and probable causes efficiently. It cannot replace SIEM-based correlation, threat
intelligence feeds, or human expert judgment for serious security events. Treat all findings as
hypotheses to be validated, not confirmed diagnoses.

6. PROBABLE FALSE POSITIVES
NAS SMB denies from 10.10.10.45: Not a security event — this is a legitimate user on Corp-
WiFi who cannot reach the NAS because the firewall policy is missing. Not suspicious behavior.
VLAN 50 IoT denies to other internal VLANs (if present): Expected and correct — VLAN 50
is isolated by design. Deny hits against internal VLANs from IoT devices are the policy working
as intended, not anomalies.
Variations and Follow-ups
1. IPS log analysis: Change the log type to "IPS" and paste IPS event logs. Ask the AI to identify
which signatures fired, classify them by severity, and recommend which to tune as false
positives versus which require immediate response.
2. Application Control log analysis: Use "Application Control" logs to identify which applications
are consuming the most bandwidth, and which to block or throttle.
3. Weekly proactive review: Change the period to "Last 7 days" and the context to "Routine
weekly log review — no known incidents." The AI will surface emerging patterns rather than
investigating a specific event.
4. Post-incident analysis: "I had a confirmed security incident on [date]. Analyze these logs
from the 48 hours before the incident to identify early indicators of compromise."
5. Log-to-firewall-rule mapping: "For each blocked connection marked as legitimate, generate
the FortiOS 7.4 CLI command to create the corresponding allow policy."
⚠  Warning / Safety Note
AI log analysis is a support tool — not a definitive security diagnosis. AI models can identify
patterns and surface probable causes efficiently, but they cannot access threat intelligence
databases, correlate across multiple data sources, or guarantee that all anomalies are identified.
Never treat AI-identified "probable false positives" as confirmed benign without independent
validation.
Repeated IPS alerts from the same internal source IP — especially an IoT device — should never
be dismissed without investigation. A device making hundreds of failed connection attempts to an
unknown external IP is not normal behavior, even if all connections are blocked. Block first (isolate
the device at the switch port level), investigate second.
Do not paste logs containing personally identifiable information (PII), client credentials, or
sensitive data into a commercial AI service. Use log sanitization tools to redact usernames, email
addresses, or personal data before analysis. Consider on-premises AI model options for
environments with strict data handling requirements.
3.5 Pre-Go-Live Checklist for Network Installations
Difficulty: Intermediate
(Requires: ability to access the management console or CLI of each device in the installation;
familiarity with the services being deployed)

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 77 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
When to Use
Use this prompt immediately before handing over a new or updated network installation to a client
or declaring it production-ready. It generates a comprehensive, structured checklist covering
every OSI layer and every service in scope — so nothing is forgotten under the pressure of a go-
live cutover window.
This prompt is especially valuable in two situations: (1) new site installations where you are
handing off to a client or internal operations team for the first time, and (2) significant
infrastructure updates (new VLANs, new services, firmware upgrades) where regression testing
across all existing services is required before signing off.
The generated checklist is designed to be completed in sequence during the cutover window.

The Prompt
You are a network commissioning engineer. Generate a comprehensive pre-go-live checklist
for the following installation. The checklist must be detailed enough to be used as an
official acceptance document.
INSTALLATION TYPE: [INSTALL-TYPE: "new site — complete installation" / "network upgrade" /
EQUIPMENT IN SCOPE: [EQUIPMENT-LIST: list all hardware to be verified]
SERVICES TO VERIFY: [SERVICES-TO-VERIFY: e.g., DHCP for all VLANs, inter-VLAN routing, Wi-
VoIP quality, IP cameras/NVR, internet connectivity, VPN/remote access, management access]
VLANs: [VLANS: list VLAN ID, name, and subnet for each]
EXPECTED CONCURRENT USERS: [EXPECTED-USERS]
CUTOVER WINDOW: [CUTOVER-WINDOW: available time and any hard constraints]
Generate a structured checklist organized into these eight areas. For each checklist item:
- Test method: exact command or physical action
- Expected result: what "PASS" looks like (specific, not vague)
- Sign-off field: who verifies (Network Engineer / Client IT / End User)
AREA 1 — HARDWARE: cabling, power, PoE, LEDs, console access
AREA 2 — LAYER 2: VLANs created, trunks active, STP stable, no loops
AREA 3 — LAYER 3: routing functional, DHCP leases, all gateways reachable
AREA 4 — SERVICES: Wi-Fi (each SSID), VoIP call quality, cameras streaming, internet
AREA 5 — SECURITY: firewall policies, changed default passwords, management access restric
AREA 6 — INTERNET AND DNS: connectivity, DNS resolution, speed test baseline
AREA 7 — END-USER TEST: realistic simulation with a client PC/phone
AREA 8 — DOCUMENTATION: what must be delivered to the client before handover
At the end, add:
- A pre-cutover checklist (what to verify before making changes)
- A rollback trigger: "If [condition], stop and revert because [reason]"

ROLLBACK TRIGGERS
Stop and revert if any of the following occur:
- STP detects a topology change loop that does not resolve within 60 seconds
- More than 20% of test hosts fail to obtain DHCP addresses after 15 minutes
- VoIP call quality is consistently below acceptable (choppy, one-way audio) and the cause
cannot be found in 30 minutes
- Internet access is completely down and ISP confirms no outage on their side
Variations and Follow-ups
1. Network upgrade (not new site): Change install type to "Upgrade: added VLAN 70 Guest-
WiFi to existing site." The checklist focuses on regression testing all existing VLANs plus
validating the new one.
2. Firmware upgrade checklist: Change to "Firmware upgrade for sw-core-01 (DGS-1210-52TP,
firmware X.X.X →  Y.Y.Y)." The prompt generates a pre-upgrade backup + verification + post-
upgrade regression test sequence.
3. Post-go-live 24-hour review: "The installation went live 24 hours ago. Generate a post-go-
live review checklist to check for any issues that emerged in the first day."
4. Tailored for a client IT handover: "The checklist will be completed by the client's internal IT
manager, not a network engineer. Adjust language and detail level accordingly — explain what
each test does in plain English."
5. Convert to PDF delivery document: Use prompt 5.2 to wrap this checklist in a formal SOP,
then use prompt 5.1 to include it in the infrastructure documentation package.
⚠  Warning / Safety Note
Document all test results before going live — not after. If a client disputes that services were
working at the time of handover, your only evidence is documented test output (screenshots, CLI
output, photos). A verbal "it all worked" is not defensible. For each checklist item, capture and date-
stamp the evidence.
Do not compress the checklist under time pressure. Go-live windows are stressful, and the
temptation to skip "obvious" steps is strong. The most damaging post-go-live issues are almost
always caused by a step that seemed obvious — default password still set on a device, one VLAN
not isolated correctly, one SSID not mapped to the right VLAN. The checklist exists precisely
because obvious steps get skipped.

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 83 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
Have rollback steps ready before you start. Every cutover that touches production infrastructure
should have a documented rollback procedure with a clear decision point: "If [condition] at [time],
we revert." Decide this before the cutover window opens, not in the middle of a problem.
3.6 Performance Diagnostics: Latency, Packet Loss, and
Slowness
Difficulty: Advanced
(Requires: ability to run iperf3, Wireshark, and basic SNMP queries; access to firewall and switch
management consoles; understanding of how to read traceroute output)
When to Use
Use this prompt when users report general slowness, video-conferencing problems, application
latency, or intermittent connectivity, and you need to systematically identify whether the
bottleneck is on your LAN, the WAN/ISP link, or an application server.
The most critical input is test data collected during the problem, not after it. Performance
problems diagnosed retrospectively from memory are almost never resolved correctly. If the
problem is intermittent, schedule a test session during a known slow period and collect
measurements as the problem is occurring.
This prompt is also suitable for proactive capacity planning: run it once a quarter with current
utilization data to identify whether you're approaching bandwidth or hardware limits before users
start complaining.

# On svc-syslog-01 (10.10.60.50) or any server — run iperf3 server:
iperf3 -s
# On an affected VLAN 40 workstation — run client:
iperf3 -c 10.10.60.50 -t 30 -P 4     # 30-second test, 4 parallel streams
# Expected if LAN is healthy: ≥ 900 Mbps (gigabit network)
# Problem indicator: < 400 Mbps on a gigabit network →  LAN issue (duplex, cable, switch)
If iperf3 shows ≥ 900 Mbps LAN throughput even during peak hours, the LAN is NOT the
bottleneck. Focus entirely on the WAN/ISP.
nload — Real-time WAN utilization on FortiGate
# FortiGate CLI — monitor WAN interface utilization in real time:
diagnose netlink interface list wan1
# or
diagnose sys top 2    # refresh every 2 seconds, check CPU and memory
# More targeted — interface bandwidth stats:
fnsysctl cat /proc/net/dev    # shows bytes/packets per interface since boot
Take a reading at off-peak (baseline) and at peak. Calculate the difference per second to get
Mbps.
Wireshark — identify which application/VLAN consumes most WAN bandwidth

Variations and Follow-ups
1. Problem is only on Wi-Fi: Add "All affected users are on VLAN 10 Corp-WiFi; wired VLAN 40
users are fine." Focus shifts to AP capacity (too many clients per radio), co-channel
interference, or WLC/AP firmware issue.
2. Problem is only in one application: "Only Microsoft Teams is slow; Zoom works fine; basic
internet is fast." Focus shifts to application-specific QoS, DSCP marking, and whether Teams
media is being routed through a relay.
3. Intermittent and hard to catch: "Problem occurs randomly 2–3 times per week; no consistent
time pattern." This points toward a flapping interface, ISP routing instability, or a scheduled
task on the network. Ask the AI for continuous monitoring recommendations.
4. After adding new devices: "Problem started when we added 15 IoT devices to VLAN 50."
Focus on whether IoT devices are generating unexpected traffic (multicast storm, constant
cloud sync, DHCP exhaustion).
5. Generate a performance baseline report: "Using the test results above, generate a one-page
network performance baseline report suitable for sharing with management."
⚠  Warning / Safety Note
Collect performance data during the problem — not before or after. A speed test run at 07:30 AM
before the office fills up tells you nothing about a peak-hour problem. Schedule a test session
deliberately during the slow period and collect all measurements simultaneously. A network that
"works fine at 8 PM" tells you nothing about what happens at 10 AM.
Do not assume the problem is your infrastructure without eliminating the ISP. Many network
engineers spend hours reconfiguring internal equipment when the problem is ISP contention. The
decision tree above is designed to eliminate ISP as a cause first. If the traceroute shows high latency
at your ISP's first router (not at your firewall), open a ticket with the ISP immediately and ask for a
line quality report.
VoIP jitter and latency targets: Per ITU-T G.114, one-way end-to-end delay ≤ 150 ms is acceptable
for toll-quality voice; ≤ 400 ms is the maximum before conversations become difficult. Jitter ≤ 30 ms
is a widely used practical target for IP voice. These are working guidelines, not absolute norms —
your PBX vendor may have different specifications. [VERIFY against current ITU-T G.114 and your
PBX vendor's documentation]

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 90 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
CATEGORY 4 — NETWORK SECURITY
5 prompts covering security posture assessment, firewall policy design, switch hardening,
incident response, and periodic access review.
4.1 Network Security Assessment (Posture Audit)
Difficulty: Intermediate
(Requires: knowledge of your current device configuration and services; familiarity with CIS
Controls and NIST CSF concepts; ability to answer honestly about current security state)
When to Use
Use this prompt for a structured self-assessment of your network's security posture — at
minimum every six months, or after any significant infrastructure change (new device, new
service, topology change, personnel change in the IT team). It produces a prioritized vulnerability
list, gap analysis against industry frameworks, and a 90-day remediation roadmap.
This prompt is a starting point for your security program, not an endpoint. It covers the
foundational questions: are defaults changed, are management interfaces restricted, are insecure
protocols disabled, is logging active? These are the controls that CIS Controls v8.1 and NIST CSF
2.0 identify as the highest-impact, lowest-effort improvements for most small-to-medium
environments.
It is not a penetration test. An AI-assisted self-assessment cannot discover exploitable
vulnerabilities, test actual attack paths, or certify your security posture. For environments
handling sensitive data or critical services, commission a professional penetration test from a
certified firm (CREST, OSCP, CEH certified) annually.
