⚠  Warning / Safety Note
This is a methodological self-assessment tool — not a penetration test. AI-assisted self-
assessment cannot discover exploitable vulnerabilities, test actual attack chains, or certify your
security posture. It surfaces known-configuration weaknesses based on what you report. An
attacker may find vulnerabilities you did not think to mention, or that require active exploitation to
detect. For organizations handling sensitive data, healthcare records, payment card data, or critical
infrastructure, commission a professional penetration test annually from a certified firm (CREST,
OSCP/OffSec, CEH certified).
Answer the context questions honestly. The quality of the assessment output is directly
proportional to the accuracy of your inputs. Optimistic answers ("I think the password was
changed") produce false reassurance. Accurate answers ("I'm not sure — I should check") produce
actionable findings.
Framework references: CIS Controls v8.1 and NIST CSF 2.0 are referenced in this prompt. [VERIFY
current version of CIS Controls at cisecurity.org/controls — v8.1 may have been superseded. VERIFY
NIST CSF 2.0 at nist.gov/cyberframework — released February 2024, verify no subsequent
revision.]
4.2 FortiGate Firewall Policy Design (Least Privilege)
Difficulty: Advanced
(Requires: FortiOS 7.4 familiarity; clear understanding of which VLANs need to communicate with
which; ability to execute CLI commands on fw-edge-01; verify syntax for other FortiOS versions at
docs.fortinet.com)
When to Use
Use this prompt when you need to design and implement a complete FortiGate firewall policy set
from scratch, or audit and rebuild an existing ruleset, following the deny-all / allow-by-exception
principle (minimum privilege). It is most powerful when you have a clear list of which VLANs exist
and exactly which traffic flows are authorized — rather than a vague "allow what works."

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 97 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
The prompt produces a complete policy table (every rule, source, destination, service, and
action), the corresponding FortiOS CLI commands to create each rule, and a verification
procedure to confirm each rule is working as intended. It also handles implicit deny placement
and generates a dedicated management-only policy.
FortiOS 7.4 note: All CLI commands in this prompt are written for FortiOS 7.4 syntax. Verify against
your firmware version at docs.fortinet.com. Key differences exist between 7.0, 7.2, 7.4, and 7.6 for
VLAN interface naming, policy UUID handling, and application control integration. [VERIFY: FortiOS
7.4 admin guide at docs.fortinet.com/product/fortigate/7.4]

        set subnet 10.10.60.10 255.255.255.255
    next
    # [add entries for 10.10.60.11, 10.10.60.20, 10.10.60.30, 10.10.60.50]
end
config firewall addrgrp
    edit "all_device_mgmt_ips"
        set member "mgmt-fw-edge-01" "mgmt-sw-core-01" "mgmt-sw-core-02" "mgmt-wlc-01"
    next
end
config firewall policy
    edit 1
        set name "Mgmt-Only"
        set srcintf "vl60-mgmt"
        set dstintf any
        set srcaddr "all"
        set dstaddr "all_device_mgmt_ips"
        set service "SSH" "HTTPS"
        set action accept
        set nat disable
        set logtraffic all
    next
    edit 2
        set name "Block-Any-to-Mgmt"
        set srcintf any
        set dstintf "vl60-mgmt"
        set srcaddr "all"
        set dstaddr "all"
        set service "ALL"
        set action deny
        set logtraffic all
    next
    edit 6
        set name "Internal-to-WAN"
        set srcintf "vl40-internal"
        set dstintf "wan1"
        set srcaddr "all"
        set dstaddr "all"
        set service "HTTP" "HTTPS" "DNS"
        set action accept
        set nat enable
        set logtraffic utm
    next
    # [continue for all remaining policies in table order]
end
3. IMPLICIT DENY (Policy 99)
The implicit deny is the last rule in the policy list. FortiGate has a built-in implicit deny for all
unmatched traffic, but making it explicit (Policy 99) allows you to log all denied traffic.

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 102 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
config firewall policy
    edit 99
        set name "Implicit-Deny-All"
        set srcintf any
        set dstintf any
        set srcaddr all
        set dstaddr all
        set service ALL
        set action deny
        set logtraffic all
        set comments "Explicit catch-all deny — must remain last in policy list"
    next
end
Rationale: Logging the implicit deny is valuable for two reasons: (1) it surfaces legitimate traffic
you forgot to allow, which you will see in the logs as blocked; (2) it captures lateral movement
attempts by compromised devices, which will appear as repeated deny hits from internal IPs.
4. VERIFICATION PROCEDURE
# FortiOS 7.4 — Packet sniffer to verify traffic is reaching the firewall:
diagnose sniffer packet vl50-iot "host 10.10.40.1" 4 0 l
# (4 = show MAC, IP, ports; 0 = capture all; l = UTC timestamps)
# Flow trace — see which policy is matching a specific flow:
diagnose debug reset
diagnose debug flow filter addr 10.10.50.15
diagnose debug flow filter daddr 10.10.40.1
diagnose debug flow show function-name enable
diagnose debug enable
diagnose debug flow trace start 50    # trace 50 packets
# Then trigger the traffic from 10.10.50.15 →  10.10.40.1
# Read the output: look for "find policy X" →  "action=deny/accept"
diagnose debug disable
# Verify policy hit counters (confirm a policy is being matched):
get firewall policy | grep -A 3 "edit 5"    # check policy 5 byte/packet counters
5. MANAGEMENT POLICY (see Policy 1 above)
Policy 1 is the dedicated management rule. Key design decisions:
- Source: vl60-mgmt ONLY — no other VLAN can initiate management sessions
- Destination: specific IP addresses of each managed device (not "all") — reduces exposure if
other IPs in VLAN 60 are compromised
- Services: SSH and HTTPS only — Telnet, HTTP, SNMP v1/v2c are NOT permitted through this
policy
Variations and Follow-ups
1. Allow SNMP monitoring from management VLAN: Add to allowed flows: "VLAN 60 →  all
devices: SNMP v3 (UDP 161) — for monitoring only." Generate the corresponding address
objects and policy.
2. Inter-site VPN: "Add a site-to-site IPSec VPN with a remote branch (10.10.80.0/24). Which
policies need to be added?"
3. FortiGate GUI steps instead of CLI: "Replace the CLI output with GUI navigation steps for
FortiOS 7.4 for administrators who prefer the web interface."

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 103 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
4. Policy audit — existing ruleset: "Here is my current policy list [paste]. Identify redundant
rules, shadow rules (rules that can never be reached), and policies missing logging."
5. Application control integration: "Add FortiGate Application Control profiles to the IoT-to-WAN
and CorpWiFi-to-WAN policies to block social media and P2P file sharing during business
hours."
⚠  Warning / Safety Note
Policy order is critical — first match wins. In FortiGate (and all stateful firewalls), policies are
evaluated top-to-bottom. A broad allow rule placed before a specific deny rule makes the deny rule
unreachable. Always put specific rules above general rules. Always put explicit denies above the
broad allow rules they override. Always put the management-only rule as Policy 1.
Test every new policy with real traffic — not just an assumed "traffic path." FortiGate's built-in
packet sniffer (diagnose sniffer packet) and flow trace (diagnose debug flow) are powerful
tools for verifying that a policy is actually matching what you expect. Do not assume a policy works
because it was syntactically correct. Use the verification procedure above for every policy before
moving to the next.
Do not delete the implicit deny before your allow rules are in place. If you clear the policy table to
rebuild it, traffic will flow freely until you re-add the deny. Work in a maintenance window or test on a
non-production firewall first.
4.3 Managed Switch Hardening
Difficulty: Intermediate
(Requires: console or SSH access to the switch; physical access recommended for applying
management access changes; test in a maintenance window)
When to Use
Use this prompt when you need to reduce the attack surface of a managed switch by disabling
insecure protocols, restricting management access, enabling Layer 2 security features, and
ensuring logging is functional. Apply it to every managed switch in your network — new
installations before go-live, existing switches during a security review, and any switch that has
never had a formal hardening pass.
This is one of the highest-value, lowest-cost security improvements available for a network that
has been set up quickly and may have been left with default or minimal configuration. Most
common switch compromises exploit exactly the vulnerabilities this prompt addresses: default
credentials, open Telnet access, and no logging.
For D-Link DGS-1210 series switches (sw-core-01, sw-core-02), all commands reference the DGS-
1210 CLI. For Cisco IOS-XE, Aruba CX, or other platforms, specify your switch model and the AI
will adapt the commands accordingly.

Page 104 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco

The Prompt
You are a network security specialist. I need to harden the following managed switch to
reduce its attack surface.
> Important: Apply hardening in a maintenance window with physical console access. Some
> changes (disabling HTTP, changing the management VLAN) will disconnect your web/GUI
> session. Always have console access before starting. Prepare a rollback procedure.
SWITCH: [SWITCH-BRAND-MODEL]
FIRMWARE: [FIRMWARE-VERSION: or "not verified" — add a note to verify at vendor site]
CURRENT ACTIVE MANAGEMENT SERVICES: [ACTIVE-MGMT-SERVICES]
MANAGEMENT ACCESS CURRENTLY ALLOWED FROM: [MGMT-ACCESS-SOURCE]
LAYER 2 PROTOCOLS ACTIVE: [L2-PROTOCOLS-ACTIVE]
SYSLOG/LOGGING SERVER: [SYSLOG-SERVER]
Generate a hardening checklist. For each item, provide:
[Current config] →  [Risk mitigated] →  [Command or procedure to apply fix]
AREA 1 — MANAGEMENT ACCESS:
- Disable Telnet (cleartext — transmits credentials in the clear)
- Disable HTTP management (cleartext — replaces with HTTPS)
- Enable SSH v2 with a strong password policy
- Restrict management access to VLAN 60 (management VLAN only) and/or specific admin IPs
AREA 2 — MANAGEMENT PROTOCOLS:
- HTTP →  HTTPS (enforce encrypted web GUI)
- SNMP v1/v2c →  SNMPv3 (with SHA-256 or SHA-512 auth and AES-128 or AES-256 privacy)
  [Note: MD5 and DES are deprecated for SNMPv3 per NIST SP 800-131A Rev 2 (2019) —
  do not use them even if the switch offers them as options]
  [VERIFY: your switch vendor's SNMPv3 cipher support in its CLI/GUI reference]
- NTP with authentication (to prevent time-tampering that invalidates log timestamps)
AREA 3 — LAYER 2 PORT SECURITY:
- Port security: MAC address limit per access port
- BPDU Guard: enabled on all access (end-device) ports — prevents rogue switch attacks
- Root Guard: enabled on non-root-bridge uplink ports — prevents unauthorized root bridge 
- DHCP Snooping: trust only the uplink port toward the DHCP server
  [VERIFY: DHCP snooping support on your switch model and firmware version — not all
  D-Link DGS-1210 firmware versions support DHCP snooping; check CLI reference]
AREA 4 — VLAN SECURITY:
- Native VLAN -= VLAN 1 (VLAN 1 default = VLAN hopping risk)
- Prune unused VLANs from trunk ports (remove any VLAN not needed on the trunk)
- Disable all unused switch ports and assign them to an isolated dummy VLAN
AREA 5 — LOGGING AND MONITORING:
- Configure syslog to [SYSLOG-SERVER]
- Enable AAA/RADIUS for centralized admin authentication (if supported)
- Set logging level to record authentication attempts, config changes, and port state chan
AREA 6 — FIRMWARE:
- Current version: [FIRMWARE-VERSION]
- Procedure for safe firmware update: backup config, schedule window, verify checksum,
  confirm rollback capability
After the checklist, add:
- Top 3 risks if this hardening is NOT applied
- Recommended hardening verification steps after applying each area

SNMPv3 Auth/Privacy Protocol Note: NIST SP 800-131A Rev 2 (2019) disallows MD5 for
authentication and DES for encryption, both of which are legacy options in some switch firmware.
Always select SHA-256 or SHA-512 for authentication and AES-128 or AES-256 for privacy when
configuring SNMPv3. If your firmware only supports MD5/DES, prioritize a firmware update before
enabling SNMPv3 — using deprecated ciphers provides weaker protection than expected. [VERIFY:
NIST SP 800-131A Rev 2 at csrc.nist.gov and your specific switch firmware's SNMPv3 cipher
options]

# Set logging to capture authentication, config changes, and port events
# Log levels: Emergency, Alert, Critical, Error, Warning, Notice, Informational, Debug
# Recommended minimum: Informational (captures most operational events)
AAA/RADIUS: If a RADIUS server is available (e.g., FreeRADIUS on svc-syslog-01), configure
centralized authentication: all admin logins authenticate against RADIUS before the local
password. This creates a central audit log of all management sessions. [VERIFY: DGS-1210
RADIUS client CLI and supported auth methods]
AREA 6 — FIRMWARE
Firmware update procedure:
1. Check current version: show switch or GUI →  System →  Device Information →  Firmware
2. Download latest firmware from https://www.dlink.com/en/support — search for DGS-1210-
52TP — verify the checksum against the vendor download page
3. Back up current configuration: GUI →  Tools →  Save/Backup Settings
4. Schedule a maintenance window (no production traffic during update)
5. Upload firmware via GUI →  Tools →  Firmware Update
6. Verify: confirm version after reboot; re-test all VLAN and management access
7. Document: date, version from/to, who performed, any issues
TOP 3 RISKS IF HARDENING NOT APPLIED
1. Credential interception via Telnet/HTTP: Any workstation with a packet capture tool
(Wireshark, tcpdump) can capture switch admin passwords from cleartext management
sessions — gaining full control of the switch configuration.
2. Rogue device takeover via default SNMP community: The "public" read-only and "private"
read-write SNMP v2c communities are universally known. An attacker on any reachable
network can query and potentially reconfigure the switch via SNMP if the firewall does not
block UDP 161.
3. Undetected changes: Without syslog, there is no record of who logged into the switch, what
was changed, or when. A compromised switch or an insider threat making unauthorized
changes will leave no forensic trail.
Variations and Follow-ups
1. Cisco IOS-XE variant: Change the switch model to "Cisco Catalyst 9300 (IOS-XE 17.x)." The
prompt will generate interface range GigabitEthernet 1/0/1-48, spanning-tree bpduguard
enable, ip dhcp snooping, and snmp-server user [user] [group] v3 auth sha [password]
priv aes 128 [priv-password] syntax.
2. Aruba CX variant: Specify "Aruba CX 6200F." Output will use ArubaOS-CX CLI: spanning-
tree bpdu-guard, snmp-server vrf default user [user] [community] v3 auth sha512 [auth-
pass] priv aes256 [priv-pass].
3. Post-hardening verification checklist: "Generate a 10-step verification checklist to confirm
that each hardening step was applied correctly, suitable for use as a test record."

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 110 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
4. Hardening for an access switch (not a core switch): "This is an access-layer switch with only
end-user ports, no trunks. Generate a simplified hardening checklist focused on port security
and management access."
5. Integrate with SOC/SIEM: "The switch logs will be forwarded to our SIEM. What log formats
and severity levels should be configured, and what types of switch events should generate
alerts?"
⚠  Warning / Safety Note
Apply hardening with physical console access. Do not start without it. Disabling HTTP, changing
the management VLAN, or restricting management to specific IPs will immediately disconnect your
current web or SSH session if those changes are applied in the wrong order. Physical console
access via USB-to-Serial cable provides a fallback that cannot be broken by network configuration
changes.
Prepare a rollback procedure before starting. Back up the switch configuration before making any
change. Know exactly how to restore the backup if a change causes an unexpected problem. The
procedure should be tested — not just written down.
SNMPv3 cipher selection: Always use SHA-256 or SHA-512 for authentication and AES-128 or
AES-256 for privacy. MD5 and DES are deprecated per NIST SP 800-131A Rev 2 (2019) and must
not be used even if the switch firmware offers them. If your firmware only supports MD5/DES for
SNMPv3, prioritize the firmware update before enabling SNMP at all. [VERIFY: NIST SP 800-131A
Rev 2 at csrc.nist.gov; check your switch vendor's current firmware for supported SNMPv3 ciphers]
DHCP snooping support varies by firmware version on D-Link DGS-1210 switches. Do not assume
it is available — verify in your specific firmware version's CLI reference before attempting to
configure it. [VERIFY: D-Link DGS-1210-52TP CLI Reference Guide for your firmware version at
dlink.com/support]
4.4 Security Incident Response Plan
Difficulty: Advanced
(Requires: access to FortiGate CLI and logs, physical access to switch ports, Wireshark on a
management workstation; for serious incidents, engage a certified IR professional)
When to Use
Use this prompt when a security incident is in progress or has just been detected: an IoT device
generating anomalous traffic, an unauthorized access attempt detected in firewall logs, a
suspected compromised workstation, or unusual network behavior that suggests active intrusion.
The prompt produces an eight-phase structured response plan tailored to your specific incident.
It prioritizes containment without evidence destruction — one of the most common mistakes
during incident response is taking actions that erase the evidence needed to understand what
happened.
Use it as soon as possible after detection — the first 15 minutes of an incident are the most critical
for preserving evidence and containing impact.

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 111 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
This is a first-response framework, not a substitute for professional incident response. For
confirmed breaches involving personal data, critical systems, or potential regulatory notification
obligations, engage a CISO or a certified incident response professional (GIAC GCIH, GCFE, or
equivalent) immediately.

[SYSTEMS-INVOLVED: list all devices, VLANs, and services that are affected or potentially

[AVAILABLE-TOOLS: what you have access to right now — FortiGate CLI, syslog server, physic
switch access, Wireshark, endpoint tools]
CRITICALITY OF AFFECTED SYSTEMS:
[CRITICALITY: production-critical / workstation-only / non-critical IoT]
Generate a structured eight-phase response plan:
1. IMMEDIATE ACTIONS (0–15 minutes): containment steps that do NOT destroy evidence;
   sequence matters — specify exact order
2. ISOLATION: how to isolate the affected segment or device at the network level without
   taking down unaffected services; specify exact switch port or VLAN actions
3. EVIDENCE COLLECTION: what to capture, from which source, and in which order;
   include chain-of-custody notes if this may involve law enforcement or litigation
4. ROOT CAUSE ANALYSIS: methodology to determine how and when the breach occurred;
   include FortiGate log queries and syslog review steps
5. ERADICATION: how to remove the threat and close the attack vector; do not restore
   until eradication is confirmed
6. RESTORATION: how to safely return systems to production with verification
7. POST-INCIDENT: documentation required, lessons learned process, preventive changes
8. COMMUNICATION: who to notify internally; regulatory notification obligations —
   provide jurisdiction-specific guidance for UK and US:
   UK: UK GDPR Article 33 / Data Protection Act 2018 — if personal data may be involved,
   notify the ICO (ico.org.uk) within 72 hours of becoming aware of a breach likely to
   risk individuals' rights and freedoms. Notify affected individuals if high risk.
   Refer to ICO breach notification guidance at ico.org.uk/for-organisations/report-a-brea
   US: No single federal 72-hour notification rule. Notify per applicable laws:
   - State breach notification laws: all 50 US states have breach notification laws;
     timelines range from "most expedient time" to 72 hours (e.g., Washington, Florida,
     some others). Consult your state's specific law.
   - HIPAA (if PHI involved): notify HHS within 60 days; notify individuals within 60 days
     notify media if breach affects >500 residents of a state.
   - SEC rules (if a publicly traded company): cybersecurity incident disclosure may be
     required under SEC rules adopted 2023. [VERIFY: current SEC cybersecurity disclosure
     requirements at sec.gov]
   - Unauthorized computer access: may trigger CFAA (18 U.S.C. § 1030) notification or
     reporting obligations; consult legal counsel.
   IMPORTANT: This is general information only, not legal advice. Consult a qualified
   attorney and your organization's privacy counsel for jurisdiction-specific obligations.

INCIDENT TYPE: IoT device generating anomalous outbound traffic to unknown external IP
INDICATORS:
- Source: 10.10.50.15 (VLAN 50 IoT), MAC: aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff (smart thermostat - Brand X)
- Traffic: HTTPS (TCP 443) bursts to external IP 185.45.223.14 — unknown/unverified
- Volume: ~300 connection attempts in 5 minutes, all blocked by FortiGate policy 12
- First detected: 2026-10-15 09:14 via FortiGate log review
SYSTEMS INVOLVED:
- VLAN 50 IoT (10.10.50.0/24) — thermostat at .15, 11 other IoT devices on same VLAN
- nvr-cam-01 (10.10.20.5) — different VLAN; same manufacturer as thermostat, uncertain
- fw-edge-01 (FortiGate 60F) — managing all policies; logs flowing to svc-syslog-01
AVAILABLE TOOLS:
FortiGate CLI (FortiOS 7.4), svc-syslog-01 (rsyslog at 10.10.60.50), physical access to
sw-core-01 (port 47 = thermostat port), Wireshark on admin workstation
CRITICALITY:
IoT VLAN 50 devices: non-critical (thermostats, smart plugs)
nvr-cam-01: critical (24/7 camera recording for security; must not be taken offline)

policy (all IoT →  internal denied; only HTTP/HTTPS to internet allowed, and that's blocked

  FortiGate GUI →  Log & Report →  Traffic →  filter: srcip=10.10.50.15, last 24h →  export CS

  Less reliable than port disable; device could spoof MAC.

   FortiGate: diagnose log filter srcip 10.10.50.0/24 | grep 185.45.223.14

→  Treat as confirmed C2 beacon. Proceed to Phase 5 (Eradication).

→  The outbound deny policy is blocking legitimate device updates. Consider adding a specif
  allow rule or review with security team before allowing. Close incident.

If confirmed C2 / compromised device:
1. Factory reset the thermostat (do not reconnect to network first; reset via physical but
2. Update firmware to latest version from vendor before reconnecting
3. Create a FortiGate blocklist entry for 185.45.223.14:
   config firewall address →  edit "C2-ip-185.45.223.14" →  set subnet 185.45.223.14/32
   →  Add to firewall policy explicit deny before any IoT allows
4. Review all other VLAN 50 devices for similar traffic patterns in the last 30 days
5. Check whether nvr-cam-01 (same manufacturer) has contacted any suspicious IPs
PHASE 6 — RESTORATION

1. Verify factory reset completed (check all settings are at defaults)
2. Verify firmware is latest available from vendor
3. Re-add to VLAN 50 — re-enable port 47 on sw-core-01: config ports 47 state enable
4. Monitor traffic for 24 hours after reconnection: sniffer packet vl50-iot "host [new-ip]
5. Confirm no recurrence of the anomalous traffic pattern
PHASE 7 — POST-INCIDENT
Write Post-Incident Report (PIR) using prompt 5.4 within 48 hours of resolution
Document: timeline, indicators, isolation method, root cause, corrective actions, preventive
measures
Lessons learned: was the IoT firewall policy effective? (Yes — blocked all traffic.) Was
detection timely? Was the syslog review that found this the only detection method? (If so: add
automated alerting for this pattern.)
Preventive change: add FortiGate alert rule for any IoT device making >50 connection
attempts to the same external IP in 5 minutes
PHASE 8 — COMMUNICATION
Internal notification (immediate):
Notify IT Manager and relevant stakeholders that an IoT device showed anomalous behavior and
has been isolated. No production impact confirmed. Investigation underway.
Regulatory notification (personal data considerations):
If personal data may be involved in this incident:
UK: Under UK GDPR Article 33 and the Data Protection Act 2018, if this incident constitutes a
personal data breach that is likely to result in a risk to individuals' rights and freedoms, notify the
ICO within 72 hours of becoming aware of the breach. Refer to ICO guidance at ico.org.uk/for-
organisations/report-a-breach/. If high risk to individuals, also notify affected individuals under UK
GDPR Article 34.
US: There is no single universal 72-hour federal notification requirement. Your obligations depend
on: (a) which state(s) the affected individuals reside in — all 50 US states have breach notification
laws with varying timelines and thresholds; (b) whether PHI is involved (HIPAA: 60-day
notification to HHS; notify individuals within 60 days; media notification if 500+ state residents);
(c) whether your organization is a public company (SEC cybersecurity disclosure requirements
adopted 2023 [VERIFY current requirements at sec.gov]).

For this specific incident (IoT device, no confirmed personal data access): The current
indicators do not confirm a personal data breach — the device was attempting outbound
connections that were blocked by the firewall. However, if investigation reveals the device was
also exfiltrating data from the network, reassess notification obligations immediately.
⚠  This is general information only — not legal advice. Regulatory notification obligations are
complex, jurisdiction-specific, and carry significant penalties for non-compliance. Engage your
privacy counsel and a qualified attorney before making notification decisions.
Variations and Follow-ups
1. Malware on a workstation: Change incident type to "Windows 11 workstation on VLAN 40
(Internal) showing signs of malware: high CPU, unexpected network traffic to multiple external
IPs." Isolation now targets endpoint containment rather than IoT port isolation.
2. Unauthorized SSH access: "Unauthorized SSH login to fw-edge-01 detected in event logs
from an external IP (203.0.113.45 — RFC 5737 TEST-NET). Last admin session was legitimate
2 days ago." Focus shifts to forensic analysis of FortiGate event logs and VPN session review.
3. Data exfiltration: "Large outbound data transfer (8 GB in 20 minutes) detected from VLAN 40
workstation 10.10.40.77 to external IP. Corporate data may be involved." This triggers
elevated regulatory notification urgency.
4. Tabletop exercise: "Convert this incident response plan into a 2-hour tabletop exercise for our
IT team. Create 5 decision points where the team must choose the next action."
5. Post-incident PIR template: "Use the Phase 7 findings above to generate a formal Post-
Incident Report using the template in prompt 5.4."
⚠  Warning / Safety Note
Do not act alone on serious security incidents. Network incidents involving confirmed data breach,
ransomware, or external threats require expertise beyond basic IR response. Engage a CISO or a
certified incident response professional (GIAC GCIH, GCFE, or equivalent). Acting alone on a serious
breach — particularly making premature restoration decisions — can eliminate legal defenses and
worsen the outcome.
Regulatory notification requirements vary significantly by jurisdiction. Do not assume a universal
72-hour rule. In the UK, UK GDPR Art. 33 / DPA 2018 imposes a 72-hour ICO notification requirement
where the breach is likely to risk individuals' rights and freedoms. In the US, there is no single
federal 72-hour rule — obligations depend on state, industry, and data type. Engage your privacy
counsel before any regulatory notification. This is general information only — not legal advice.
Evidence preservation before containment. The instinct in an incident is to "fix it fast." Resist this.
Taking containment actions before capturing evidence (logs, packet captures, network state)
eliminates the ability to understand the root cause — guaranteeing that the same attack succeeds
again. Evidence first; containment second (within reason — obvious active data exfiltration is an
exception).

4.5 Periodic Network Device Access Review
Difficulty: Intermediate
(Requires: access to each network device's management console to verify account lists;
willingness to change passwords immediately during the review)
When to Use
Use this prompt every three to six months to audit who has access to your network devices, with
what privileges, and through which methods. Over time, every network accumulates orphaned
accounts (former employees, contractors, temporary projects), excessive privileges (accounts
that were given admin access "temporarily"), and insecure methods (passwords that have never
been changed, Telnet still enabled "for legacy compatibility").
This prompt is most effective when combined with a completed device inventory table. Fill in the
table honestly — including the uncomfortable rows (password never changed, default account
still present) — and the output will give you a prioritized action plan.
The review takes approximately 30–60 minutes per device to complete. Budget a half-day for a
full network review of 6–8 devices.
Required Inputs
Placeholder What to provide Example
[DEVICE-
INVENTORY]
Table of all devices with account information
(see format in prompt)
See worked example — fill one
row per device
Expected Output
Risk analysis: accounts and access methods that create specific, named risks
Accounts to disable or modify immediately, with technical rationale and priority
Configuration corrections ordered by severity
Periodic review plan: recommended cadence, procedure, and checklist
Updated access inventory template to maintain as a living operational document

Recommended cadence: Every 6 months (formal review) + immediately after any of the trigger

Standard review procedure:
1. Collect the access inventory table (update this document before starting)
2. For each device: log in and export the local account list; compare with the table
3. Verify each account is still needed: confirm active users; disable dormant accounts
4. Verify password age: if any account password is older than 6 months →  schedule change
5. Verify access method: confirm Telnet is disabled; confirm HTTP is disabled; confirm SSH v2 is
the only CLI method

- Any IT staff member or contractor with device access leaves the organization →  change all
device passwords within 24 hours
- Security incident involving any network device →  full access review before returning to normal
operations
- Firmware update on a device →  verify that update did not reset any settings to defaults
- New device added to the network →  complete hardening (prompt 4.3) before connection to
production

Variations and Follow-ups
1. Post-staff-departure review: "One IT administrator left the company 2 days ago. They had
access to all devices in the inventory above. Generate an emergency access review and
password rotation plan."
2. Integrate with RBAC: "Redesign the access model to implement role-based access control:
Network Admin (full), NOC Operator (read-only), Auditor (log access only). Generate the
account configuration for each role on each device."
3. Automate the review: "Using the Python 3.11+ automation pattern (see prompt 6.2), write a
script that SSHes to each device, retrieves the local account list, and flags any account not in
the approved list."
4. Expand to wireless clients: "Add a wireless access review: which SSID credentials have been
shared with which users? When were they last rotated? Which users still need guest access?"
5. Compliance report: "Using the access inventory above, produce a one-page access control
compliance summary for management, citing CIS Controls v8.1 Control 5 (Account
Management)."
⚠  Warning / Safety Note
Accounts with passwords that have never been changed on critical devices are a severe
vulnerability — especially if the password is a vendor default or was shared with former
employees. Change these immediately in a maintenance window. Document who knows the new
credentials and store them in an encrypted password manager or secrets vault — never in a plaintext
spreadsheet.
Password rotation on production devices requires care. Changing credentials on a switch or
firewall remotely can lock you out if SSH access breaks mid-change. Always have console access
before changing management passwords on production devices.
Orphaned accounts are a persistent risk. When an IT staff member leaves, every password they
knew — not just their personal account — must be changed. If they had the admin password for
your firewall or switches, those passwords are now potentially in the possession of a party outside
your organization.

5.1 Complete Network Infrastructure Documentation
Difficulty: Beginner
(Requires: the ability to describe your infrastructure accurately; no CLI commands needed — this
prompt generates documentation from your description)
When to Use
Use this prompt whenever you need to produce structured technical documentation for an
existing network — for client handover, new-technician onboarding, internal audit, or simply to
create a reference document that doesn't exist yet. The prompt generates a complete, structured
document from your plain-language infrastructure description.
It adapts the output level to the intended audience: a highly technical audience gets CLI-level
detail; management gets a 5-line executive summary and flow charts; a client IT team gets
something in between.
This is also useful when you have taken over a network and need to document what's there
before making any changes. Describe what you find, run the prompt, and produce a formal
infrastructure record.

flowchart TD
    ISP["ISP — 100 Mbps Fiber"] -->|WAN| FW["fw-edge-01<br->FortiGate 60F<br->10.10.60.1"]
    FW -->|Trunk port 52, VLANs 10-70| SW1["sw-core-01<br->D-Link DGS-1210-52TP<br->10.10.
    SW1 -->|Trunk port 51, VLANs 40,60| SW2["sw-core-02<br->D-Link DGS-1210-52<br->10.10.6
    SW1 -->|Trunk port 44, VLANs 10,70| WLC["wlc-01<br->D-Link DNH-1000<br->10.10.60.20"]
    WLC -->|"CAPWAP tunnel"| AP["ap-floor1-01<br->DAP-X3060<br->10.10.60.30"]
    SW1 -->|"PoE, VLAN 20 access"| NVR["nvr-cam-01<br->10.10.20.5"]
    SW1 -->|"VLAN 30 access"| PBX["pbx-voip-01<br->10.10.30.5"]
    SW1 -->|"VLAN 60 access"| SVC["svc-syslog-01<br->10.10.60.50"]
    SW2 -->|"VLAN 40 access ports"| PC["Workstations<br->VLAN 40"]
    AP -->|"VLAN 10"| CW["Corp-WiFi clients"]
    AP -->|"VLAN 70"| GW["Guest-WiFi clients"]
Note: Mermaid diagrams generated by AI may contain syntax errors in complex topologies. Always
test in the live preview at mermaid.live before including in documentation. Both graph TD and
flowchart TD syntax are valid; flowchart TD is preferred in Mermaid v10+.
5. KEY POLICIES
Firewall: All inter-VLAN traffic is controlled by fw-edge-01 (FortiGate 60F). Deny-all is the default;
traffic is permitted only by explicit allow rules. Key policies:
- VLAN 40 (Internal) →  WAN: HTTP, HTTPS, DNS — wired workstations may reach internet
- VLAN 10 (Corp-WiFi) →  VLAN 40 (Internal): allowed for file server and office resources access
- VLAN 20 (Cameras) →  NVR 10.10.20.5 only: cameras communicate only with their NVR
- VLAN 50 (IoT) →  WAN: limited HTTP/HTTPS only; no internal VLAN access

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 127 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
- VLAN 70 (Guest) →  WAN only; client isolation prevents guest-to-guest communication
- VLAN 60 (Mgmt) →  management IPs only: SSH and HTTPS only; no other source VLAN can
initiate management sessions
QoS: VLAN 30 (VoIP) is marked DSCP EF (Expedited Forwarding, DSCP 46) at the phone port
level; sw-core-01 is configured for strict priority on CoS 5 for VoIP traffic. Target: one-way delay
≤ 150 ms, jitter ≤ 30 ms.
Remote Access: SSL-VPN on fw-edge-01, TCP 443, with MFA. Two administrator accounts
permitted.
Logging: All network devices log to svc-syslog-01 (10.10.60.50). FortiGate event and traffic logs
are retained for [RETENTION-PERIOD-PLACEHOLDER] days. Log review is scheduled
[FREQUENCY-PLACEHOLDER].
6. EMERGENCY PROCEDURES
If primary management workstation is unavailable:
1. Use console cable (USB-to-Serial, RJ45-to-DB9 adapter for D-Link) to connect directly to the
console port of any network device
2. PuTTY settings: COM port = [check your laptop's Device Manager], Speed = 115200, Data bits
= 8, Stop bits = 1, Parity = None, Flow = None
3. Login with admin credentials stored in [CREDENTIAL-STORE-PLACEHOLDER]
If internet access is down:
1. Check fw-edge-01 dashboard: Is the WAN interface UP? Is the ISP IP assigned?
2. If WAN interface is DOWN →  call ISP: [ISP-CONTACT-PLACEHOLDER]
3. If WAN interface is UP but no internet →  check FortiGate routing table: get router info
routing-table all — default route should point to ISP gateway
4. Internal network (email, file server, VOIP between internal extensions) continues to work during
an internet outage
Escalation contacts:
| Role | Name | Contact |
|---|---|---|
| Primary Network Admin | [PLACEHOLDER] | [PLACEHOLDER] |
| Secondary / Backup | [PLACEHOLDER] | [PLACEHOLDER] |
| ISP Support | [ISP-NAME] | [ISP-SUPPORT-NUMBER] |
| FortiGate Support | Fortinet TAC | support.fortinet.com / [CONTRACT-NUMBER] |

8. NOTES FOR THE SUCCESSOR

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 128 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
FortiGate interface names: The FortiGate creates one sub-interface per VLAN (named vl10-
corpwifi, vl20-cameras, etc.). These interface names are referenced in firewall policies —
changing them requires updating every policy that references them.
D-Link DGS-1210 web GUI timeout: The web GUI session times out in 5 minutes. Long CLI
sessions via SSH are more reliable for making multiple changes.
NVR camera recording: nvr-cam-01 records 24/7 to local storage. Storage capacity is
approximately [STORAGE-SIZE-PLACEHOLDER]. When the disk fills, oldest recordings are
overwritten. Check disk usage monthly.
WLC controller and APs: All access points must be managed through wlc-01. Do not try to
configure ap-floor1-01 directly via its IP — it is in "managed" mode and will not accept local
configuration changes.
Syslog server: svc-syslog-01 is a Linux server running rsyslog. It also serves as the NTP
source for all network devices. If this server goes down, NTP synchronization for all devices
will be lost. This will cause log timestamp drift and may trigger certificate validity errors.
Document must be updated whenever a device is added, removed, or reconfigured. Outdated
documentation can be worse than no documentation — a technician acting on incorrect
documentation can cause an outage.
Variations and Follow-ups
1. Executive-only version: Change audience to "CEO/Management" and detail level to "Low."
Output will be a one-page business summary with no IP addresses, CLI commands, or VLAN
IDs.
2. IT team onboarding guide: Change audience to "new IT technician (first day)" and ask the AI
to add a "How to access and monitor this network" quick-start guide.
3. Audit preparation: Add to the context: "This documentation will be reviewed by an external
security auditor." The prompt will add compliance-relevant annotations.
4. Update an existing document: "Here is our existing network documentation: [paste]. Identify
what is outdated or missing and generate the updated sections."
5. Client delivery package: "Convert this documentation into a formal client delivery document
with a cover page, table of contents, and a section for the client to sign confirming receipt."
⚠  Warning / Safety Note
Documentation has value only if it is current. A document that was accurate 18 months ago and has
never been updated is unreliable. Every network change — new device, IP change, VLAN addition,
firmware update — must be reflected in this document before the technician closes the change
ticket. Build the update step into your standard change procedure (see prompt 5.2 for SOPs).
Do not share this document via unencrypted email. It contains the full network topology, all
management IP addresses, and potentially device access information. Treat it as a sensitive internal
document. Store it in an access-controlled location and restrict who can view it.
Mermaid diagrams generated by AI require verification. Complex topologies with many nodes
frequently have syntax errors in AI-generated Mermaid code. Always paste the output into
mermaid.live to verify it renders correctly before including it in any published document.

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 129 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
5.2 Write a Standard Operating Procedure (SOP)
Difficulty: Beginner
(Requires: knowledge of the procedure being documented; no CLI access needed — this prompt
generates text from your description)
When to Use
Use this prompt to standardize any repeatable network procedure: adding a new switch,
onboarding an AP, rotating device passwords, applying firmware updates, adding a new VLAN.
Without a written SOP, every technician executes the same task differently — missing steps,
introducing inconsistencies, or creating problems that require an experienced person to
troubleshoot.
SOPs are also a risk management tool: they allow a less-experienced technician to execute a
procedure correctly the first time, and they provide a reference for training new staff without
losing tribal knowledge when experienced technicians leave.
Write this prompt for the most error-prone or highest-impact procedures first — the ones where a
mistake would cause a production outage or security incident.
Required Inputs
Placeholder What to provide Example
[PROCEDURE-
NAME]
Name of the procedure "Adding a New Managed Switch to the Corporate
Network"
[TRIGGER] When is this procedure
executed?
"Every time a new managed switch is added to the
network"
[ROLES] Who performs it, who
approves it?
"Network Engineer (executor), IT Manager (prior
approval)"
[TOOLS-
NEEDED]
Equipment and software
required
"Console cable (USB-to-Serial), laptop with
PuTTY, IP documentation, FortiGate CLI access"
[ESTIMATED-
TIME]
How long should it take? "2 hours including verification"
[MAIN-STEPS] High-level list of what the
procedure does
"Unbox, physical install, console config, VLAN
setup, trunk config, verification, documentation"
[SYSTEMS-
IMPACTED]
What could be affected if
something goes wrong?
"Zero impact if on new ports; risk of trunk
disruption if connecting to core switch"
Expected Output
A formal SOP document with eight sections: header, purpose/scope, prerequisites, numbered
procedure with decision points, verification, rollback, troubleshooting, and version history.

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 130 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
The Prompt
You are an IT documentation specialist. Write a formal Standard Operating Procedure (SOP)
for the following procedure.
PROCEDURE NAME: [PROCEDURE-NAME]
TRIGGER: [TRIGGER: when is this procedure executed? e.g., "every time a new switch is adde
ROLES:
- Executor: [EXECUTOR-ROLE, e.g., "Network Engineer"]
- Approver: [APPROVER-ROLE, e.g., "IT Manager — approval required before execution"]
TOOLS REQUIRED: [TOOLS-NEEDED: physical tools, software, credentials, documentation needed
ESTIMATED EXECUTION TIME: [ESTIMATED-TIME]
MAIN STEPS (high-level): [MAIN-STEPS: numbered list of the major phases]
SYSTEMS IMPACTED: [SYSTEMS-IMPACTED: what could be disrupted if something goes wrong]
Generate a formal SOP with these eight sections:
1. HEADER: Title, SOP number, version (1.0), creation date (fill [DATE-PLACEHOLDER]),
   author ([AUTHOR-PLACEHOLDER]), approved by ([APPROVER-PLACEHOLDER]), status (Draft/Acti
2. PURPOSE AND SCOPE:
   - Purpose: what this SOP accomplishes in 2–3 sentences
   - In scope: what procedures this SOP covers
   - Out of scope: what this SOP does NOT cover (to prevent scope creep)
3. PREREQUISITES: everything that must be confirmed as ready before Step 1 begins —
   equipment, access, documentation, approvals, change window
4. PROCEDURE: fully numbered steps with sub-steps. Include:
   - Decision points: "If [condition] →  go to Step X.Y / stop and consult"
   - Expected output at each step (what does success look like?)
   - Notes or cautions where mistakes are most likely
5. VERIFICATION: how to confirm the procedure succeeded end-to-end —
   specific tests with expected results
6. ROLLBACK: how to revert to the previous state if something goes wrong —
   step-by-step rollback procedure; do not leave this section vague
7. TROUBLESHOOTING: the 3 most common problems encountered when executing this procedure,
   with their probable cause and resolution
8. VERSION HISTORY:
   | Version | Date | Author | Change Summary |
   |---------|------|--------|----------------|
   | 1.0     | [DATE] | [AUTHOR] | Initial version |
At the end: add a "REVIEW SCHEDULE" note: "This SOP must be reviewed every 12 months or
after any significant infrastructure change that affects the procedure."
Worked Example
Sample Input (fills all placeholders with reference network values):

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 131 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
PROCEDURE NAME: Adding a New Managed Access Switch to the Corporate Network
TRIGGER: Every time a new managed switch is added to Corp.Example Lab network
ROLES:
  Executor: Network Engineer
  Approver: IT Manager (written approval required in ticketing system before work begins)
TOOLS REQUIRED: Console cable (USB-to-Serial, RJ45-to-DB9), laptop with PuTTY installed,
  current IP Addressing document, FortiGate CLI access (SSH from VLAN 60 workstation),
  D-Link DGS-1210 documentation (firmware version of new switch), cable tester
ESTIMATED EXECUTION TIME: 2.5 hours including verification
MAIN STEPS:
  1. Obtain approval and assign IP/hostname in IP addressing document
  2. Unbox and physical installation in rack
  3. Console cable initial configuration (hostname, VLAN, trunk, management IP, SSH, syslo
  4. Connect to core network (trunk link to sw-core-01 or sw-core-02)
  5. Verify VLAN propagation and DHCP through new switch
  6. Apply security hardening checklist (see SOP: Switch Hardening)
  7. Update network documentation
  8. Notify IT Manager of completion
SYSTEMS IMPACTED:
  Zero impact if new switch is on new ports with no existing traffic.
  Risk of VLAN disruption if trunk is connected to sw-core-01 during production hours.
Sample Output (illustrative — actual AI output will vary):
CORP.EXAMPLE LAB — STANDARD OPERATING PROCEDURE
Field Value
Title Adding a New Managed Access Switch to the Corporate Network
SOP Number SOP-NET-001
Version 1.0
Status Draft
Created [DATE-PLACEHOLDER]
Author [AUTHOR-PLACEHOLDER]

2. PURPOSE AND SCOPE
Purpose: This SOP defines the standard procedure for adding a new managed switch to the
Corp.Example Lab network. It ensures consistent configuration, VLAN propagation, security
hardening, and documentation for every new switch deployment.
In scope: Adding a new access-layer managed switch (D-Link DGS-1210 series or equivalent) to
an existing trunk infrastructure.
Out of scope: Replacing an existing core switch (separate SOP required); firmware upgrades to
existing switches; adding unmanaged switches (not permitted on this network); wireless access
point deployments.
3. PREREQUISITES

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 132 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
Before starting Step 1, confirm:
- [ ] Change request approved in ticketing system by IT Manager (attach ticket number: ___)
- [ ] New IP address and hostname allocated in IP Addressing document (assign in VLAN 60
range: 10.10.60.x)
- [ ] New switch and patch cables physically available in the server room
- [ ] Console cable (USB-to-Serial, RJ45-to-DB9) and laptop with PuTTY available
- [ ] Access to D-Link DGS-1210 CLI Reference Guide for the new switch's firmware version
- [ ] SSH access to sw-core-01 or sw-core-02 (from VLAN 60 admin workstation) — for trunk
configuration
- [ ] Maintenance window confirmed: for trunk connection, only during low-traffic hours or out-of-
hours
4. PROCEDURE
Step 1 — Documentation pre-work (15 min)
1.1. Open the IP Addressing document. Record:
- New switch hostname: ___ (e.g., sw-access-floor2)
- Assigned management IP: 10.10.60. (next available in VLAN 60)
- Physical location: __
- Ticket number: _______
1.2. Determine which VLANs the new switch will carry (based on which user types will connect):
- [ ] VLAN 40 Internal (wired workstations) — almost always required
- [ ] VLAN 10 Corp-WiFi (if APs will be connected)
- [ ] VLAN 30 VoIP (if VoIP phones)
- [ ] VLAN 20 Cameras (if cameras)
- [ ] VLAN 60 Mgmt (always required for management access)
1.3. Record the trunk uplink: will this switch trunk to sw-core-01 (PoE) or sw-core-02 (non-PoE)?
- Decision: if the new switch will power PoE devices →  choose sw-core-01; if no PoE needed →
sw-core-02
Step 2 — Physical installation (20 min)
2.1. Mount switch in rack. Secure with rack screws.
2.2. Connect power; verify power LED is solid green.
2.3. Connect console cable to switch console port.
2.4. Open PuTTY: Serial, COM [your port number], 115200 baud, 8N1, no flow control.
2.5. Press Enter to get a login prompt. Default credentials: [CHECK: D-Link DGS-1210 default
credentials — documented in CLI Reference Guide — change IMMEDIATELY in Step 3.2].
Step 3 — Initial console configuration (30 min)
3.1. Set hostname:
# D-Link DGS-1210 CLI — [VERIFY exact commands in firmware CLI Reference Guide]
config system_name [NEW-HOSTNAME]
3.2. Change admin password:
config account admin password [NEW-STRONG-PASSWORD]
⚠  Record the new password in the credential vault BEFORE moving to the next step.

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 133 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
3.3. Create all required VLANs:
create vlan vlanid 10 name Corp-WiFi
create vlan vlanid 40 name Internal
create vlan vlanid 60 name Mgmt
# [add others as determined in Step 1.2]
3.4. Configure management IP address (VLAN 60):
config ipif System ipaddress 10.10.60.[X]/24 vlan 60
config ipif System gateway 10.10.60.1
3.5. Enable SSH, disable Telnet and HTTP:
enable ssh
disable telnet
# Keep HTTPS enabled; disable HTTP
[VERIFY: D-Link DGS-1210 SSH enable and Telnet disable CLI commands]
3.6. Configure syslog:
config syslog host ipaddress 10.10.60.50 severity informational state enable
3.7. Configure NTP:
config sntp primary 10.10.60.50
3.8. Save configuration to NVRAM:
save
Step 4 — Configure trunk uplink (20 min)
4.1. Identify the uplink port (physically — the port you will cable to sw-core-01 or sw-core-02).
4.2. Configure the uplink port as a trunk — tagged for all required VLANs, untagged for VLAN 60
(management native):
config vlan vlanid 10 add tagged [UPLINK-PORT]
config vlan vlanid 40 add tagged [UPLINK-PORT]
config vlan vlanid 60 add untagged [UPLINK-PORT]
config port_vlan [UPLINK-PORT] pvid 60
# [repeat for other VLANs in scope]
4.3. On sw-core-01 or sw-core-02: add the new switch's uplink port to trunk configuration (same
VLANs).
4.4. Connect the patch cable between the new switch uplink port and the core switch port.
4.5. Verify link up: both port LEDs solid green.
Step 5 — VLAN and DHCP verification (20 min)
5.1. Connect a test laptop to a VLAN 40 access port on the new switch.
5.2. Run ipconfig /renew — expected result: IP in 10.10.40.50–.200 range.
5.3. Ping the gateway (10.10.40.1): expected <5 ms, 0% loss.

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 134 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
5.4. Ping internet (8.8.8.8): expected < 30 ms from LAN.
5.5. SSH to the new switch from management workstation (10.10.60.x): confirm management
access works.
5.6. Decision point: If any of the above tests fail →  STOP. Do not proceed. Diagnose using
prompts 3.1 or 3.2. Do not connect additional devices until all verification steps pass.
Step 6 — Security hardening
6.1. Apply switch hardening checklist (SOP-SEC-001 or prompt 4.3). Minimum requirements:
- Default accounts removed or disabled
- Management access restricted to VLAN 60 IPs only
- BPDU Guard enabled on access ports
- Syslog confirmed flowing to svc-syslog-01
Step 7 — Documentation (15 min)
7.1. Update the IP Addressing document: add new switch hostname, IP, model, firmware, physical
location.
7.2. Update the Device Inventory in the Network Documentation (prompt 5.1 document).
7.3. Record a Change Log entry: date, author, switch added, IP assigned, trunk connected to,
ticket number.
7.4. Update network topology diagram if applicable.
Step 8 — Notify completion
8.1. Update the ticketing system: mark ticket as resolved; attach a one-line completion note.
8.2. Notify IT Manager of completion (email or ticket comment).

6. ROLLBACK
If anything goes wrong during this procedure:
1. Disconnect the patch cable between the new switch and the core switch (remove from
production immediately if causing issues)

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 135 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
2. The new switch is now isolated — no traffic impact on the rest of the network
3. Review the configuration on the new switch via console
4. Re-check each step in Section 4 — the most common errors are: incorrect PVID on trunk port;
wrong VLAN tagged/untagged assignment; management IP in wrong VLAN
5. Do not reconnect to the core switch until all verification tests in Section 5 pass on an isolated
test

REVIEW SCHEDULE: This SOP must be reviewed every 12 months or after any significant
infrastructure change that affects the procedure (e.g., change of switch vendor, change of VLAN
scheme, change of management IP addressing).
Variations and Follow-ups
1. Different procedure: Replace the procedure name with any other repeatable task: "Rotating
admin passwords on all network devices," "Onboarding a new Wi-Fi access point," or
"Applying a firmware update to sw-core-01."
2. Multiple SOPs from one session: "Using the format above, generate SOPs for these three
procedures: [list]." The AI will produce three complete SOPs in one response.
3. Simplified quick-reference card: "Convert SOP-NET-001 above into a one-page quick-
reference card for technicians — bullet points only, no narrative, suitable for printing and
laminating."
4. Procedure review: "Here is our existing SOP for [procedure]: [paste]. Review it and identify:
missing steps, steps that are vague or ambiguous, missing rollback procedure, and anything
outdated."
5. Translate to another language: For the Italian edition — "Translate this SOP to Italian,
maintaining formal technical language throughout."

⚠  Warning / Safety Note
An SOP has no value until it has been tested. Walk through the procedure on a non-production
device or in a lab environment before declaring it official. An untested SOP that references incorrect
commands, missing steps, or incorrect device behavior gives technicians false confidence — they
will follow the procedure correctly and still produce the wrong result.
Always include a rollback procedure — even for "simple" changes. The most disruptive network
changes are the ones everyone thought were routine. If you cannot write a rollback procedure for a
step, that step is not well-understood enough to automate into an SOP. Figure out the rollback first,
then finalize the SOP.
Keep the credential step explicit and security-conscious. Any SOP that involves credentials must
document how those credentials are stored (password vault, not plaintext), and must not include
actual credential values in the SOP text itself.
5.3 Monthly Network Status Report
Difficulty: Beginner
(Requires: actual monitoring data — uptime, incident logs, ticket counts, change records;
estimates should not be used)
When to Use
Use this prompt at the end of each calendar month to generate a structured status report for
management, a client, or an MSP account review. It transforms raw operational data (uptime
percentage, incident count, ticket metrics, changes made) into a professional narrative with an
executive summary and KPI dashboard.
The quality of this report depends entirely on the quality of the input data. If you do not yet have
a monitoring tool generating uptime and latency metrics, this prompt will remind you — but it
cannot substitute for real data. A report built on estimates or guesses has no operational or
contractual value.

1. Firewall firmware review (P1 — business risk): The fw-edge-01 FortiGate 60F has not had a

1. MSP multi-client edition: "Adapt this report template for an MSP delivering monthly reports
to multiple clients simultaneously. Include an MSP header/footer, client branding placeholder,
and a separate section for SLA compliance status against contractual thresholds."
2. Weekly status snapshot: "Generate a condensed 1-page weekly network status update using
the same structure but with a shorter format — executive summary + KPI table only, with a
'Notable events this week' bullet list instead of full incident and change log sections."

Reference specific contractual uptime thresholds (99.5% monthly, 99.9% annually), calculate
whether SLA penalties apply, and include a formal SLA credit section if applicable."
4. Security-enhanced edition: "Add a Network Security section to this report covering: firewall
policy changes made this month, count of blocked connection attempts (by category if
available from FortiGate logs), security incidents (with severity), and open security
recommendations from last month."
⚠  Warning / Safety Note
Warning: Report data must come from real monitoring systems — not estimated from memory,
extrapolated from sparse notes, or approximated because "that's roughly what it was." An
inaccurate uptime figure or fabricated MTTR value in a report used to demonstrate SLA compliance
creates contractual risk and destroys the trust that monthly reporting is designed to build. If you do
not currently have a monitoring system collecting historical availability and performance data (uptime
per device, alert history, ticket timestamps), deploy one first — Zabbix, Grafana + Prometheus,
Checkmk, or a commercial equivalent — before adopting this reporting process. A report built on
guesses has no operational or contractual value.

5.4 Post-Incident Report (PIR) with Root Cause Analysis
Difficulty: Intermediate
(Requires: incident timeline documentation, basic understanding of 5-Whys methodology, access
to monitoring logs and change records)
When to Use
Use this prompt within 48–72 hours of closing any significant network incident — any event that
required immediate action, caused measurable user impact, triggered an alert, or consumed more
than 30 minutes of team investigation. The PIR transforms reactive incident response into
institutional knowledge, converting raw incident data into structured findings that drive
permanent improvements.
This prompt generates a formally structured PIR suitable for internal knowledge bases
(Confluence, SharePoint, Notion), SOC 2 and ISO 27001 audit trails, SLA documentation, and
change management input. The embedded 5-Whys root cause analysis systematically traces the
incident from symptom to systemic cause — identifying not just what broke, but why the system
allowed it to break.
Adopt a proactive PIR culture: the prompt is equally valuable for medium-impact P3 incidents
where a near-miss revealed a latent vulnerability that could have been a P1 under different
circumstances. If the incident taught the team something, it deserves a PIR.
Not a substitute for: the live incident response process (which happens during the incident), the
change management process for implementing the preventive actions the PIR identifies, or team
retrospectives focused on broader process improvement.

The Prompt
You are an IT incident manager applying the blameless post-mortem principle.
Write a formal Post-Incident Report (PIR) for the incident below.
Use ONLY the data provided — do not invent timelines, causes, or impacts.
INCIDENT:
- Title: [INCIDENT-TITLE]
- Start: [START-TIMESTAMP] | End: [END-TIMESTAMP]
  Calculate and state total duration in minutes and hours.
- Severity: [SEVERITY] | Type: [INCIDENT-TYPE]
IMPACT:
[IMPACT-DESCRIPTION — include: number of users affected, systems/services unreachable,
business operations disrupted, SLA implications, estimated cost if available]
CHRONOLOGICAL TIMELINE:
[List events in HH:MM — actor — event format, e.g.:
09:15 — Zabbix — Alert: multiple VLAN 40 hosts unreachable
09:22 — Helpdesk — First user call logged
09:30 — Network Engineer — SSH session opened to sw-core-01
--.]
ROOT CAUSE (technical description):
[What caused the incident — be specific: configuration change, hardware failure, software
defect, human error, or process gap]
REMEDIATION APPLIED:
[Actions taken to restore service:
- [Action] — performed by [ACTOR] at [TIME]
- [Action] — --.]
Write a formal PIR with these 8 sections:
1. EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
   — Non-technical language; maximum 5 lines
   — Cover: what happened, who was affected, how it was resolved, current status
   — Written for a non-technical business audience (IT manager, client)
2. DETAILED TIMELINE
   — Formatted table: Time | Actor | Event
   — Calculate and highlight the detection lag (time from incident start to first alert)
   — Note any gaps where no actions were logged
3. ROOT CAUSE ANALYSIS — 5-WHYS METHOD
   — Work through 5 Why questions systematically from symptom to systemic cause
   — Identify both the proximate cause (what broke) and the underlying systemic cause
     (why the system allowed this to happen and go undetected)
   — Add a text-format Fishbone diagram with categories: People | Process | Technology | E
   — If the 5-Whys terminates at a human error, continue asking: what process or system
     safeguard should have prevented that error from having this impact?
4. DETAILED IMPACT ASSESSMENT
   — Technical: systems, services, data affected
   — User: count, roles, operations disrupted
   — Business: processes halted, revenue/productivity risk (estimate if provided)
   — SLA: uptime calculation, breach or near-breach status
5. REMEDIATION — APPLIED AND PLANNED
   — Table: Action | Owner | Status (Applied / Planned) | Date Applied | Deadline
   — Include both immediate restoration actions and longer-term preventive work
6. LESSONS LEARNED
   — What the team did well (acknowledge strengths explicitly)
   — What could have been detected or resolved faster
   — Gaps between the actual response and the ideal response
7. PREVENTIVE ACTIONS
   — Specific, measurable changes to prevent recurrence

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 145 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
   — Map each action to a specific finding from the Root Cause Analysis
   — Table: Action | Maps to RCA Finding | Owner | Target Date
8. APPROVAL TEMPLATE
   — Technical Owner sign-off block (name, signature, date)
   — IT Management sign-off block (name, signature, date)
   — Document version: 1.0 | Classification: Internal Confidential
Apply the blameless post-mortem principle throughout: every finding in the Root Cause
Analysis must point to a process, technology, or organizational gap — never to individual 
If the 5-Whys terminates at "the engineer made a mistake," continue: why did the system
allow that mistake to have this impact? That is the real root cause.

1. Executive Summary
On October 15, 2026, approximately 45 staff members lost access to internal network services —
file servers, the ERP application, and network printers — for 90 minutes starting at 09:15. The
incident was caused by a configuration error made during a testing session the previous evening:
an internal network segment (VLAN 40) was accidentally removed from a switch connection and
was not detected overnight because traffic levels were too low to trigger the monitoring system.
Once users arrived the following morning and alerted the helpdesk, the network team identified
and fixed the issue within 30 minutes. No data was lost, and no other network areas were
affected. This report documents the cause, the response, and the steps being taken to prevent a
recurrence.

Why

Why
1
Why did VLAN 40 users lose all

VLAN 40 was missing from trunk port 49 on sw-core-
01, blocking all inter-VLAN routing through fw-edge-01
Why
2
Why was VLAN 40 missing from
the trunk?
It was removed during pre-change testing on Oct 14 and
not restored before the engineer ended the session
Why
3
Why wasn't it immediately restored
or detected?
No checklist or post-test verification procedure
required confirming that all production VLANs remained
intact after a test session
Why
4
Why did monitoring not detect this
within minutes?
Monitoring alert thresholds required 3 consecutive ping
failures, which did not accumulate during the low-traffic
overnight period
Why
5
Why was the threshold traffic-
dependent rather than
configuration-state-aware?
Monitoring was configured for user-hours traffic
patterns only; there was no 24/7 baseline for critical
VLAN reachability independent of traffic volume

Proximate cause: VLAN 40 removed from trunk port during testing; not restored.
Systemic cause: Absence of a mandatory post-test VLAN verification step and traffic-volume-
independent monitoring thresholds allowed a configuration error to persist undetected for 15
hours.
4. Detailed Impact Assessment
Technical impact: VLAN 40 (10.10.40.0/24) fully isolated from inter-VLAN routing; all hosts
unable to reach other VLANs or internet through fw-edge-01
User impact: ~45 users on the Internal segment; unable to access file shares, ERP application,
and network printers for 90 minutes during business hours
Business impact: Core business operations disrupted; no transactions permanently lost (ERP
sessions resumed after reconnect); no security breach
Financial estimate: 45 users × 1.5 h × €40/h average = ~€2,700 productivity cost (internal
estimate — not a contractual figure)
SLA impact: Monthly uptime: 99.8% (87 min total downtime in October); threshold: 99.5%
(216 min allowed). No breach. However, a recurrence of similar duration would bring October
cumulative downtime to 174 min — still within threshold, with only 42 minutes of margin

6. Lessons Learned
What the team did well:
- Once alerted, root cause was identified and corrective action was applied within 30 minutes — a
strong and focused diagnostic response
- Monitoring correctly detected the issue when traffic levels made it alertable
- All helpdesk tickets were resolved and confirmed within 15 minutes of the fix being applied
What could have been better:
- A mandatory post-test VLAN verification step would have prevented the incident entirely — the
configuration error should never have persisted past the end of the Oct 14 testing session
- Monitoring thresholds that are traffic-volume-dependent create a 15-hour detection blind spot
during low-traffic periods for a production VLAN — this is unacceptable
- The detection lag between the configuration error and the first alert was 15 hours; the target for
critical VLAN outage detection should be under 5 minutes, independent of time of day
7. Preventive Actions

Variations and Follow-ups
1. Executive-summary-only version: "Generate a 1-page PIR executive brief suitable for
sharing with non-IT management. Include only: what happened, who was affected, what was
done, and what will be done differently — no technical detail."
2. MSP SLA breach version: "Adapt this PIR template for a managed service provider. Include a
formal SLA breach calculation section, SLA credit assessment, and a customer communication
draft (non-technical, factual, suitable for sending to the affected client)."
3. Security incident variant: "Reframe this PIR template for a security incident (unauthorized
access, data exposure, or ransomware response). Add sections for: evidence preservation and
forensic log retention, regulatory notification assessment (UK GDPR/ICO 72-hour rule where
applicable, US state breach notification where applicable), and third-party communication
(affected individuals, regulators)."
4. Fishbone-only variant: "Skip the 5-Whys analysis and produce only the Fishbone diagram in
Mermaid diagram syntax, formatted for embedding in Confluence or GitHub Markdown."
⚠  Warning / Safety Note
Warning: A PIR is a learning document — not a blame document. Teams that use post-incident
reviews to identify individual fault will produce increasingly inaccurate PIRs as engineers learn to omit
details that make them look bad. This eliminates the primary value of the process. Apply the
blameless post-mortem principle throughout: every finding in the Root Cause Analysis should point
to a process, technology, or organizational gap — not to a named individual's error. If your 5-Whys

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 150 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
terminates at "the engineer made a mistake," keep asking: Why did the system allow that mistake to
have this impact? What safeguard, checklist, or monitoring threshold should have caught it? That is
the real root cause. The goal is systemic improvement, not individual accountability.
5.5 IP Addressing and VLAN Allocation Document (Client
Handover)
Difficulty: Beginner
(Requires: complete IP addressing plan, list of all static IP assignments, list of all network devices
with management IPs)
When to Use
Generate this document at the close of any network installation, migration, significant
infrastructure change, or annual documentation review. It is the formal handover artifact — the
single source of truth for IP addressing and VLAN allocation that a client, a new IT manager, or a
service provider needs to manage and troubleshoot the network going forward.
This prompt replaces the informal IP spreadsheet that lives in one engineer's laptop with a
professional, versioned, classification-marked document that captures every IP address, VLAN,
and management access method in the network. The document format is deliberately structured
for longevity: version history, confidentiality notice, and review date ensure it remains useful
rather than becoming a source of confusion as the network evolves.
Use it for periodic documentation updates too — when the network has drifted from its last
documented state (new devices added, subnets changed, VLANs reconfigured) and you need to
regenerate an accurate current-state record.
Not a substitute for: a live IPAM tool (NetBox, phpIPAM, Infoblox) for large environments with
hundreds of IP assignments, a full network topology diagram (see prompt 3.6 for site survey and
topology documentation), or a change management document (see prompts 5.1 and 5.2 for
change documentation).

   — Flag any IP that falls outside the expected static range for its VLAN
   — Instruct engineer to fill in MAC addresses from device labels or switch ARP table
4. NETWORK EQUIPMENT TABLE
   — All network infrastructure devices with management access details
   — Columns: Hostname | Model | Management IP | Role | Access Method | Location
   — Flag any device that lacks a documented management IP or access method

   — Default gateway per VLAN (reference the VLAN table)
   — DNS configuration: primary and secondary
   — NTP server configuration
   — Management network access summary: VLAN, IP range, authentication method
   — Note any VLANs with internet access restrictions or special QoS policy

document is actively harmful — engineers will reference incorrect addresses and create

CATEGORY 6 — AUTOMATION & SCRIPTING
6 prompts covering Python 3.11+ network discovery, configuration backup, availability monitoring,
CLI output parsing, AI-enhanced weekly reporting, and a professional multi-vendor library. All
scripts target Python 3.11+, use English comments, load credentials from environment variables,
and include dry-run modes where appropriate.
6.1 Python Network Discovery Script
Difficulty: Intermediate
(Requires: Python 3.11+ installed, ability to run scripts with network permissions, familiarity with
reading CSV output; root/administrator privileges required on some OS for ARP operations)
When to Use
Use this prompt when you need to automatically discover all active devices across one or more IP
ranges — building or refreshing an asset inventory, identifying rogue devices, confirming which
hosts are reachable before a change window, or auditing a VLAN for unexpected occupants.
The generated script performs a multi-threaded ping sweep, ARP-based MAC address lookup,
reverse DNS hostname resolution, and a socket-based port scan — all without requiring Nmap or
other external binaries. Output is a timestamped CSV file plus a formatted console table, suitable
for importing into a CMDB or comparing against an existing asset registry.
Before running: Ensure you have explicit written authorization to scan the target network. On
Windows, ARP lookup requires the scapy library and may require administrator privileges; on
Linux/macOS, standard subprocess ARP is available but ICMP (ping) may require root. Test on a
single /32 host first before running against a full subnet.
Not a substitute for: a full vulnerability scanner (Nessus, OpenVAS), an enterprise IPAM tool
(NetBox, Infoblox), or a purpose-built network management system. This script is a lightweight
discovery tool — it detects what is reachable, not what is misconfigured or vulnerable.

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 159 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
Required Inputs
Placeholder What to provide Example
[IP-RANGES] One or more CIDR subnets to scan "10.10.10.0/24, 10.10.20.0/24,
10.10.60.0/24"
[PORTS-TO-
SCAN]
Comma-separated list of TCP ports to
probe
"22, 80, 443, 23, 161, 8080, 8443"
[OUTPUT-FILE] CSV output file path "/home/ubuntu/discovery_2026-10.csv"
[OS-
PLATFORM]
Target OS for script execution "Linux" / "Windows" / "macOS"

COUNT]
Max parallel threads (default: 50;
reduce on slow networks)
"50"

Seconds to wait per ping (default: 1.0) "1.0"
[PORT-
TIMEOUT]
Seconds to wait per port connection
(default: 0.5)
"0.5"
Expected Output
A production-quality Python 3.11+ script with:
- Multi-threaded ping sweep using ThreadPoolExecutor
- ARP-based MAC address lookup (scapy on Windows; subprocess arp -n on Linux/macOS)
- Socket-based TCP port scan (no Nmap dependency)
- Reverse DNS lookup via socket.gethostbyaddr
- CSV export with timestamp + formatted console table using tabulate
- Structured error handling: unreachable hosts, timeouts, permission errors
- argparse CLI: specify IP ranges, ports, output file, timeout, thread count
- English comments throughout
- requirements.txt with pinned versions
- Pre-run authorization check: prompts the user to confirm authorization before scanning

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 160 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
The Prompt
You are a Python network automation developer. Write a production-quality Python 3.11+
network discovery script. Use English comments throughout.
IP RANGES TO SCAN: [IP-RANGES]
INFORMATION TO COLLECT: active IP (ICMP ping), MAC address (ARP), hostname (reverse DNS),
open TCP ports: [PORTS-TO-SCAN]
OUTPUT: CSV file at [OUTPUT-FILE] + formatted table on console
TARGET OS: [OS-PLATFORM]
THREAD COUNT: [THREAD-COUNT]
TIMEOUTS: ping [PING-TIMEOUT]s, port scan [PORT-TIMEOUT]s
Generate a complete script with:
1. PRE-RUN AUTHORIZATION CHECK
   — Print a clear warning about authorized-use-only requirement
   — Prompt the user to type "I CONFIRM" before scanning begins
   — Exit immediately if confirmation is not received
2. MULTI-THREADED PING SWEEP
   — Use concurrent.futures.ThreadPoolExecutor (max_workers=[THREAD-COUNT])
   — ICMP ping via subprocess (ping -c 1 on Linux/macOS, ping -n 1 on Windows)
   — Timeout: [PING-TIMEOUT] seconds per host
   — Return: IP, alive (bool), RTT in ms
3. ARP MAC LOOKUP (alive hosts only)
   — Linux/macOS: subprocess arp -n [IP] | parse output
   — Windows: subprocess arp -a [IP] | parse output
   — Handle: ARP cache miss (return "unknown"); permission error (return "requires-root")
4. REVERSE DNS HOSTNAME LOOKUP
   — socket.gethostbyaddr() with exception handling
   — Return "no-rdns" if lookup fails — do not crash
5. TCP PORT SCAN (alive hosts only)
   — Socket connect with [PORT-TIMEOUT]s timeout per port
   — Return list of open ports as comma-separated string
   — Do not use Nmap or any external binary
6. CSV EXPORT
   — Columns: timestamp, ip, alive, rtt_ms, mac, hostname, open_ports
   — Filename: [OUTPUT-FILE] (append timestamp to filename to avoid overwrites)
   — Write header row; append rows as discovered
7. CONSOLE TABLE
   — Use tabulate library (format: "grid") for terminal output
   — Show: IP | Alive | RTT | MAC | Hostname | Open Ports
   — Print summary at end: total hosts scanned, alive count, scan duration
8. CLI INTERFACE (argparse)
   — --ranges: comma-separated CIDR ranges (required)
   — --ports: comma-separated port list (default: 22,80,443,8080)
   — --output: output CSV file path (default: ./discovery_{timestamp}.csv)
   — --threads: thread count (default: 50, max: 200)
   — --ping-timeout: seconds (default: 1.0)
   — --port-timeout: seconds (default: 0.5)
   — --skip-ports: skip port scanning (faster, ping+ARP+rDNS only)
9. ERROR HANDLING
   — Per-host exceptions caught individually — one failure does not stop the sweep
   — Log errors to stderr; continue processing remaining hosts
   — Print final error summary
10. requirements.txt
    — Pin all dependencies with exact versions
    — Include: scapy, tabulate, ipaddress (stdlib in 3.11)
