⚠  Warning / Safety Note
Warning: Predictive site survey software provides estimates, not measurements. Physical survey
results in real environments consistently differ from predictions — especially in concrete buildings,
stairwells, and near industrial equipment. A predictive survey is a starting point for AP placement; a
post-deployment passive survey is mandatory before signing off on coverage quality for VoIP or
other latency-sensitive applications. Do not quote coverage guarantees to clients based on
predictive modeling alone.
B.7 BGP Prefix Filtering and Route Security
Difficulty: Advanced
(Requires: BGP ASN, peer ASN, allocated IP prefix list, access to BGP router CLI, understanding
of BGP routing policy)
When to Use
Use this prompt when configuring BGP peering with an ISP or IX (Internet Exchange), hardening
an existing BGP session, or auditing BGP route filtering policies. Proper BGP prefix filtering is a
critical internet routing security control — accepting or announcing incorrect prefixes can cause
route leaks, traffic hijacking, or accidental contribution to BGP route leaks that affect the global
routing table.

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 216 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
The Prompt
You are a network security engineer specializing in BGP route security. Configure
and audit BGP prefix filtering for the session described below.
YOUR ASN: [YOUR-ASN]
PEER ASN: [PEER-ASN]
PEER TYPE: [ISP-upstream | IX-peering | customer-downstream]
YOUR ALLOCATED PREFIXES: [YOUR-IP-PREFIXES — list of CIDR blocks you own]
EXPECTED PREFIXES FROM PEER: [PEER-PREFIXES — if known; "default only" for ISP upstream]
ROUTER PLATFORM: [PLATFORM — FortiGate 60F FortiOS 7.4 | Cisco IOS-XE | other]
RPKI VALIDATION: [Enabled / Not yet enabled]
Generate:
1. INBOUND PREFIX FILTER (what you ACCEPT from the peer)
   For ISP upstream: accept default route (0.0.0.0/0) only, or full table with bogon filte
   For IX peering: accept only prefixes RPKI-valid and covered by peer's IRR records
   For customer: accept only customer's allocated prefixes (explicit list)
   ALWAYS REJECT INBOUND:
   — RFC 1918 private space (10/8, 172.16/12, 192.168/16)
   — RFC 5735 special-use space (100.64/10, 127/8, 169.254/16, 192.0.0/24, 198.18/15)
   — IPv6 bogons (-:1/128, -:/128, fc00-:/7, fe80-:/10)
   — Your own prefixes (prevent routing loop)
   — Prefixes longer than /24 for IPv4 (or /48 for IPv6) — too specific; likely leaked
2. OUTBOUND PREFIX FILTER (what you ANNOUNCE to the peer)
   — Announce ONLY your allocated prefixes: [YOUR-IP-PREFIXES]
   — Verify each prefix against your RIR allocation records before including
   — Never announce: default route (unless intentional); prefixes you do not own;
     more-specific prefixes not in your IRR records
3. RPKI CONFIGURATION
   — If RPKI not yet enabled: high priority recommendation with ROA registration steps
   — If enabled: configure route-origin validation; policy: RPKI-invalid →  reject
   — [VERIFY: FortiOS 7.4 RPKI validation support at docs.fortinet.com]
   — Register your ROAs at your RIR (ARIN for US, RIPE for UK/EU):
     [VERIFY: current RIR ROA registration process at your RIR's portal]
4. CONFIGURATION TEMPLATE
   — Platform-specific BGP prefix-list and route-map configuration
   — Include max-prefix limit: alert at 80% of expected count; hard-drop at 120%
   — FortiOS 7.4 BGP syntax [VERIFY command syntax at docs.fortinet.com]
5. BGP SECURITY HARDENING CHECKLIST
   — MD5 session authentication: configured? [Prefer TCP AO if platform supports it]
   — TTL security (GTSM): configured? (prevent off-path BGP session attacks)
   — Max-prefix limit: configured per session?
   — Route refresh capability: enabled?
   — Graceful restart: enabled for ISP sessions?
   — Monitoring: BGP session state changes in syslog + alerting?
Worked Example
Sample inbound filter (ISP upstream — accept default only):

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 217 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
# FortiOS 7.4 BGP inbound prefix filter — accept default route only from ISP
# [VERIFY exact FortiOS CLI syntax at docs.fortinet.com before deploying]
config router prefix-list
    edit "ISP-INBOUND-ACCEPT"
        config rule
            edit 1
                set prefix 0.0.0.0/0
                set exact-match enable    # [VERIFY: exact-match vs le/ge syntax]
                set action permit
            next
        end
    next
end
# All other routes from ISP are implicitly denied by default deny at end of prefix-list
⚠  Warning / Safety Note
Warning: BGP misconfiguration can cause catastrophic routing failures — potentially affecting your
entire organization's internet connectivity and contributing to global route leaks that impact other
networks. Before deploying any BGP prefix filter change in production: test in a lab environment or
using a BGP simulator; have a rollback configuration ready; coordinate with your ISP on the change;
and verify that your RPKI ROA registrations are correct before enabling RPKI-based filtering (an
incorrect ROA can cause your own prefixes to be dropped by RPKI-filtering peers). The BGP
security checklist items (MD5, TTL security, max-prefix) are baseline requirements — implement all
of them, not just the prefix filters. This prompt output is a starting configuration template — have it
reviewed by a BGP-experienced network engineer before deployment.
B.8 Agentic RAG for Network Knowledge Base
Difficulty: Advanced
(Requires: familiarity with RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) architecture, LLM API, vector
database, collection of network documentation to index)
When to Use
Use this prompt when building an AI assistant that your network team can query using natural
language to retrieve information from your internal documentation — runbooks, SOPs, network
diagrams, change records, PIRs, device configurations (redacted), and vendor documentation —
without each engineer having to search through multiple systems manually.
This is the most advanced prompt in the pack and requires significant infrastructure setup.
Approach it as a multi-week implementation project, not a single-session task.

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 218 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
The Prompt
You are an AI systems architect specializing in RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation)
systems for IT operations. Design and partially implement an agentic RAG system
for a network team knowledge base.
DOCUMENT CORPUS: [LIST-OF-DOCUMENT-TYPES — e.g., SOPs, PIRs, IP addressing tables,
                  change records, vendor documentation PDFs, Confluence pages]
QUERY USE CASES: [LIST-OF-QUERIES — e.g., "What is the rollback procedure for sw-core-01?"
                  "Which VLANs allow internet access?", "What happened during the Oct 2026
LLM: [LLM-CHOICE — self-hosted (Ollama) or cloud API]
VECTOR DATABASE: [VECTOR-DB — e.g., Chroma, Qdrant, pgvector, Pinecone]
DEPLOYMENT: [On-premise | Cloud | Hybrid]
Design:
1. DOCUMENT INGESTION PIPELINE
   — Supported formats: Markdown, PDF, Confluence pages (REST API), plain text
   — Chunking strategy for network documents:
     * SOPs: chunk by procedure step (preserve numbered step context)
     * PIRs: chunk by section (keep executive summary as standalone chunk)
     * IP addressing tables: chunk by VLAN/subnet group with full table as metadata
     * CLI configurations: chunk by device, stanza-aware (preserve interface/policy blocks
   — Metadata: source_file, document_type, device_name (if applicable), last_updated, clas
   — Security: strip credentials and sensitive strings (passwords, SNMP communities,
     API keys) before indexing — provide a pre-ingestion sanitization script
2. RETRIEVAL ARCHITECTURE
   — Hybrid retrieval: dense (semantic embedding) + sparse (BM25 keyword)
   — Re-ranking: use a cross-encoder to re-rank top-k candidates before context assembly
   — Query types:
     * Factual ("What is the IP of fw-edge-01?"): high-precision retrieval, small context
     * Procedural ("How do I add a new VLAN?"): retrieve full SOP; step-by-step response
     * Incident-related ("What was the root cause of the Oct 2026 outage?"): retrieve PIR
3. AGENT ARCHITECTURE (agentic RAG — query planning)
   — Query classifier: detect query type (factual / procedural / incident / comparison)
   — Tool routing:
     * search_kb(query, doc_type_filter) →  list[chunks]: semantic search with filter
     * get_document(doc_id) →  str: retrieve full document by ID
     * get_device_info(hostname) →  dict: structured lookup from device registry
   — Multi-hop reasoning: if first retrieval does not answer the query, decompose
     into sub-queries and retrieve additional context
4. SYSTEM PROMPT FOR RAG AGENT
   "You are a Network Operations Knowledge Base Assistant for [ORGANIZATION-NAME].
    You answer questions about the network infrastructure by retrieving and synthesizing
    information from internal documentation only.
    RULES:
    - Only answer from retrieved context — never from general AI knowledge about unrelated
    - If the answer is not in the retrieved documents, say 'I cannot find this in the
      knowledge base — please check [documentation system] or ask the network engineer'
    - Never reveal internal IP addresses, credentials, or topology details to
      users who do not have documented access to that information
    - Cite the source document and section for every factual claim
    - Flag information that may be outdated (document older than 6 months without review d
5. ACCESS CONTROL
   — Classify documents by sensitivity: Public | Internal | Confidential | Restricted
   — Implement user-level access control: users only retrieve documents they are
     authorized to access (do not mix public and confidential chunks in the same response)
   — Audit log: every query, retrieved documents, user ID, timestamp
6. EVALUATION FRAMEWORK
   — Test set: 20 representative questions with known correct answers from your documentat
   — Metrics: retrieval recall (was the answer-containing chunk retrieved?),
              answer accuracy (did the LLM produce the correct answer?),
              citation accuracy (was the cited source correct?)
   — Evaluation cadence: after every major corpus update

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 219 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
7. IMPLEMENTATION STACK (starter)
   — Ingestion: LangChain document loaders or custom Python pipeline
   — Embeddings: sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (self-hosted) or provider API
   — Vector store: Chroma (local, simple setup) or pgvector (if PostgreSQL already deploye
   — LLM: Ollama with Llama 3 (on-premise, no data leaves the building) or cloud API
   — Interface: simple Gradio or Streamlit web UI for team use
8. requirements.txt with pinned versions (starter set)

Variations and Follow-ups
1. Multi-tenant MSP edition: "Extend this RAG architecture for a managed service provider with
multiple clients — each client's documentation is indexed in an isolated namespace; users can
only query their own client's knowledge base."
2. Automated PIR ingestion: "Build an ingestion pipeline that automatically adds new PIR
documents (from prompt 5.4) to the knowledge base immediately after they are created,
updating the incident history index in real time."
⚠  Warning / Safety Note
Warning: A RAG system over your network documentation is a high-value target — it aggregates
internal IP addresses, device credentials (if not properly sanitized before indexing), topology
information, and operational procedures into a searchable store. Security requirements: (1)
implement access controls that prevent users from retrieving documents above their authorization
level; (2) run a pre-ingestion sanitization pass to strip all credentials, API keys, and SNMP
community strings before any document is indexed — use the sanitization script generated in step 1;

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 220 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
(3) host the vector database and LLM on-premise if your documentation is classified Confidential or
above; (4) audit log all queries — a user searching for "what is the firewall admin password" should
be flagged immediately; (5) never index running configuration files directly — create sanitized
summaries for indexing.

GLOSSARY OF TECHNICAL TERMS
Reader guide: This glossary covers the key networking and AI terms used throughout the prompt
pack. Definitions are written for practitioners — concise, accurate, and opinionated where a single
best practice exists. Cross-references to prompts where each concept is applied in depth are listed
in parentheses.
Networking Terms
Access Control List (ACL)
A sequential list of permit/deny rules applied to network traffic at a router, firewall, or switch
interface. Rules are evaluated top-down; the first match wins. ACLs filter traffic based on
source/destination IP, protocol, and port. In enterprise FortiGate deployments, ACLs are
implemented as firewall policies with explicit deny-all as the final rule. (See prompts 4.2, 4.3)
Border Gateway Protocol (BGP)
The inter-domain routing protocol that manages how IP prefixes are exchanged between
autonomous systems (AS) on the internet. BGP makes routing decisions based on path attributes
rather than metric. Improper BGP configuration can lead to route leaks — the inadvertent
advertisement of your customer or internal routes to upstream providers — with potentially
internet-wide consequences. (See prompt B.7)
DHCP (Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol)
A network management protocol that automatically assigns IP addresses, subnet masks, default
gateways, and DNS server addresses to client devices. In multi-VLAN environments, DHCP relay
(or a dedicated DHCP server per VLAN) is required because broadcast-based DHCP requests do
not cross VLAN boundaries. (See prompts 2.3, 3.3)
DNS (Domain Name System)
The distributed hierarchical system that resolves human-readable domain names (e.g.,
corp.example.com) to IP addresses. Split DNS — separate internal and external DNS zones — is
a common enterprise pattern to prevent internal hostnames from leaking into public DNS.
[VERIFY: Reference NIST SP 800-81-2 for DNS hardening guidelines]
Ethernet (IEEE 802.3)
The dominant wired LAN standard. Modern enterprise networks run on Gigabit Ethernet (1 Gbps,
802.3ab) and 10-Gigabit Ethernet (10 Gbps, 802.3an) over Cat 6A copper or multi-mode fiber.
The IEEE 802.3bt standard (PoE++) delivers up to 90 W per port, enabling high-power devices
such as PTZ cameras and wireless access points on a single cable. (See prompt 1.1)
FortiOS
Fortinet's operating system for FortiGate next-generation firewalls. All FortiGate CLI examples in
this pack target FortiOS 7.4. Syntax and features may differ on earlier or later releases — always
verify against your firmware version at docs.fortinet.com. (See prompts 2.3, 3.4, 4.2)
Inter-VLAN Routing
The process of forwarding traffic between logically separated VLANs. Implemented either via a
Layer 3 switch (SVI — Switched Virtual Interface) or a router-on-a-stick configuration where a

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 222 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
single physical uplink carries multiple VLANs and the router or firewall performs the routing. In the
Corp.Example Lab reference network, the FortiGate fw-edge-01 performs inter-VLAN routing for
all seven VLANs. (See prompts 1.1, 2.3)
IPAM (IP Address Management)
The discipline of planning, tracking, and managing IP address space. Enterprise IPAM tools (e.g.,
phpIPAM, NetBox, Infoblox) maintain authoritative records of subnets, assigned addresses, host
records, and VLAN bindings. Manual spreadsheet IPAM is error-prone and should be migrated to
a dedicated tool for networks larger than 50 devices. (See prompt B.5)
NVR (Network Video Recorder)
A device that receives, stores, and manages video streams from IP cameras. NVRs communicate
with cameras over a dedicated VLAN (VLAN 20 Cameras in Corp.Example Lab) that is isolated
from user-facing VLANs to prevent lateral movement if a camera is compromised. (See prompts
1.4, 4.1)
PoE (Power over Ethernet)
A technology that delivers DC power over standard Ethernet cabling simultaneously with data.
PoE (802.3af, up to 15.4 W), PoE+ (802.3at, up to 30 W), and PoE++ (802.3bt, up to 60/90 W)
are the three main standards. The sw-core-01 (D-Link DGS-1210-52TP) in the reference network
provides PoE for access points and IP phones. (See prompt 1.1)
PBX (Private Branch Exchange)
A telephone switching system for internal voice communications. Modern IP PBX systems (such
as Asterisk, FreePBX, or 3CX) use the SIP protocol over VoIP infrastructure. VoIP traffic should be
placed on a dedicated VLAN (VLAN 30 VoIP in Corp.Example Lab) with QoS prioritization to
minimize jitter and packet loss. (See prompts 1.4, 2.6)
QoS (Quality of Service)
A set of techniques for prioritizing specific traffic types to ensure acceptable performance. VoIP
and real-time video require QoS to limit jitter (< 30 ms), one-way delay (< 150 ms), and packet
loss (< 1%). DSCP (Differentiated Services Code Point) marking and scheduling policies are the
primary QoS mechanisms in modern enterprise networks. (See prompt 2.6)
RPKI (Resource Public Key Infrastructure)
A cryptographic framework that ties IP address prefixes to their registered holders, allowing
network operators to create and validate Route Origin Authorizations (ROAs). BGP route origin
validation (ROV) using RPKI rejects routes whose origin AS does not match the registered ROA,
blocking a significant class of BGP hijacking attacks. (See prompt B.7)
SD-WAN (Software-Defined WAN)
An approach to wide-area networking that uses software to centrally manage and optimize
multiple underlying transport links (MPLS, broadband, 4G/5G). SD-WAN provides application-
aware path selection, centralized policy management, and dynamic failover. FortiGate supports
SD-WAN natively from FortiOS 6.2+. (See prompt B.3)
SNMP (Simple Network Management Protocol)
A protocol for monitoring and managing network devices. SNMPv3 with authentication
(MD5/SHA) and encryption (DES/AES) is required; SNMPv1 and SNMPv2c transmit community
strings in cleartext and must not be used on production networks. All scripting examples in this
pack that use SNMP target SNMPv3. (See prompts 6.1, 6.3)
SSH (Secure Shell)
A cryptographic protocol for secure remote command-line access to network devices. SSH uses
public-key or password authentication over an encrypted channel. SSH keys should be 4096-bit

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 223 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
RSA or Ed25519; password authentication should be disabled in favor of key-based
authentication in hardened environments. (See prompts 2.7, 4.3)
STP (Spanning Tree Protocol)
A Layer 2 protocol (IEEE 802.1D and its extensions 802.1w RSTP, 802.1s MSTP) that prevents
switching loops in networks with redundant paths by selectively blocking ports. Rapid PVST+
(Cisco) or Rapid MSTP is preferred over classic STP for faster convergence. (See prompt 1.1)
Trunk Link
A switch port or physical link configured to carry traffic for multiple VLANs simultaneously using
IEEE 802.1Q encapsulation. Each Ethernet frame is tagged with a 4-byte 802.1Q header
containing the VLAN ID (1–4094). The native VLAN (untagged traffic) should always be changed
from the default VLAN 1 in production environments. (See prompts 2.1, 2.2)
VLAN (Virtual Local Area Network)
A logical network segment created within a physical switching infrastructure, defined by IEEE
802.1Q. VLANs provide traffic isolation, simplify security policy enforcement, and reduce
broadcast domain size. The Corp.Example Lab reference network uses seven VLANs (10–70)
mapped to distinct IP subnets. (See prompts 1.1, 2.1, 2.2)
Zero Trust Network Architecture (ZTNA)
A security model that replaces implicit trust based on network location with explicit, continuous
verification of every user, device, and workload before granting access to resources. The core
principle: "never trust, always verify." ZTNA requires identity-aware access controls, micro-
segmentation, and continuous session monitoring. (See prompt B.2)
Artificial Intelligence and Agentic AI Terms
Agentic AI
An AI system that operates autonomously over multiple steps to accomplish a goal, using tools
(APIs, scripts, databases) and making sequential decisions without requiring human input at
every step. Distinguished from simple chatbots by its ability to plan, use tools, and recover from
errors. (See Category 7)
Context Window
The maximum amount of text (measured in tokens) that a language model can process in a single
inference call. As of 2026, leading models support context windows ranging from 32 K to over 1
M tokens. Large context windows allow models to process complete configuration files or log
extracts, but larger inputs increase latency and cost. (See prompts 6.5, 7.4)
Fine-Tuning
The process of continuing to train a pre-trained language model on a domain-specific dataset,
adjusting its weights to improve performance on that domain. Distinct from prompt engineering
(which does not change model weights) and RAG (which retrieves external information at
inference time). Fine-tuning requires a labeled training dataset and significant compute resources.
Hallucination
The tendency of language models to generate plausible-sounding but factually incorrect or
entirely fabricated output. In network engineering contexts, hallucinations manifest as invented
CLI commands, non-existent vendor features, or incorrect RFC citations. All AI-generated network
configurations must be validated by a human professional before deployment. (Addressed in all
prompts — see warning sections)

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 224 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
Human-in-the-Loop (HITL)
A design pattern in which a human operator must review and approve AI-generated actions
before they are executed. HITL is a mandatory requirement for any agentic AI prompt in this pack
that generates device configurations, executes scripts, or modifies production infrastructure. (See
Category 7, all agentic prompts)
Kill Switch
An emergency stop mechanism that halts an autonomous agent immediately, preventing further
action. In the agentic prompts in this pack, a kill switch is implemented as a monitored stop-file
(e.g., /tmp/agent_stop) that the agent checks before every tool call. (See prompts 7.1, 7.3, 7.5)
LLM (Large Language Model)
A neural network trained on large text corpora to generate, classify, and transform text. LLMs are
the core AI component in all prompts in this pack. This pack is deliberately model-agnostic —
prompts are designed to work with any capable LLM. See the "Model Selection" note in the How
to Use section. (See Category 7)
Orchestrator
In multi-agent systems, the orchestrator is the top-level agent responsible for decomposing tasks,
assigning sub-tasks to specialized worker agents, collecting results, and producing a final output.
The orchestrator does not directly access network devices — it delegates tool use to workers.
(See prompts 7.3, 7.6)
Prompt Injection
An attack in which malicious text embedded in an external data source (a log file, a device
hostname, a ticket description) is interpreted as a new instruction by the AI model, overriding its
original system prompt. All agentic prompts in this pack include explicit prompt injection
defenses. (See Category 7, warning sections)
RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation)
A technique that augments LLM responses by retrieving relevant documents from an external
knowledge base at inference time and including them in the model's context. RAG reduces
hallucination by grounding the model's output in verified source documents. In network
operations, the knowledge base typically contains SOPs, runbooks, vendor documentation, and
incident reports. (See prompt B.8)
ReAct (Reasoning + Acting)
A prompting pattern in which the model alternates between reasoning about the current state
("Thought:"), selecting a tool action ("Action:"), observing the result ("Observation:"), and
repeating until the task is complete. ReAct is the most widely used pattern for agentic AI task
execution. (See Category 7)
System Prompt
The instruction set provided to a language model at the start of a conversation that defines its
role, behavior constraints, output format, and any persistent facts it should know. System
prompts are typically hidden from end users in production deployments. High-quality system
prompts are the primary determinant of output quality for agentic AI systems. (See prompt 7.6)
Token
The basic unit of text processed by a language model. A token is approximately 0.75 English
words (or 4 characters) on average. Cost and context window limits are measured in tokens. A
complete 50-prompt document at this level of detail is approximately 60,000–80,000 tokens.
Tool Call
An LLM action that invokes an external function, API, script, or service. Tool calls are the primary
mechanism by which agentic AI systems interact with the real world — executing scripts,

NETWORKING & AGENTIC AI PROMPT PACK — Professional Edition
Page 225 of 237 · Copyright © 2026 Luisi Francesco
querying APIs, reading files, or sending alerts. In the agentic prompts in this pack, tool calls are
logged and subject to human review before deployment. (See Category 7)

ABOUT THIS EDITION — VERSION HISTORY
About the English Edition
This is the Professional English Edition of the Networking & Agentic AI Prompt Pack, a
substantial expansion and revision of the original Italian-language edition (v1.0). The English
edition is a distinct product — not a direct translation.
What Changed from v1.0 (Italian Edition)
Structural expansions:
- Prompt count increased from 42 to 50 (eight original bonus prompts added: B.1–B.8)
- Every prompt expanded from a 5-field structure to a full 9-field structure, adding: Required
Inputs, Expected Output, Worked Example with realistic sample output, Variations/Follow-ups,
and Warning/Safety Note
- A canonical reference network (Corp.Example Lab) introduced across all prompts, replacing the
vague "Company X" placeholders of v1.0
Content corrections applied (from STAGE1_AUDIT_REPORT.md — 74 audit issues):
- IP addressing standardized to the canonical 10.10.x.x scheme throughout (v1.0 used
inconsistent 192.168.x.x ranges that conflicted between prompts)
- FortiOS CLI commands verified against FortiOS 7.4 documentation [VERIFY: confirm all
commands at docs.fortinet.com before publication]
- D-Link DGS-1210 series CLI commands updated to current firmware syntax [VERIFY: confirm at
dlink.com/support]
- All Python code updated to Python 3.11+ with type hints, environment-variable-based secrets,
and dry-run modes
- Security warnings added or strengthened in every applicable prompt
- Legal jurisdiction corrected: US edition references CFAA (18 U.S.C. § 1030) and UK CMA 1990;
Garante/D.Lgs.231/2001 references removed (Italian edition only)
- NIST CSF updated to NIST CSF 2.0; CIS Controls updated to v8.1
Category 7 (Agentic AI) — new content:
- All seven agentic prompts include mandatory HITL gates, kill switch patterns, prompt injection
defenses, cost limit enforcement, and audit logging requirements
- Model-agnostic design with dated "current models as of [DATE]" note boxes — model
recommendations do not lock users to a specific provider
Bonus section (B.1–B.8) — entirely new:
Eight prompts covering advanced topics not present in v1.0: IPv6, Zero Trust, SD-WAN, CAB
change documentation, IPAM/CMDB, Wi-Fi site surveys, BGP/RPKI, and Agentic RAG.

Professional Judgment Required
AI-generated output may be wrong. Large language models can generate plausible-sounding
but factually incorrect information — a phenomenon called "hallucination." In the context of
network engineering, this may manifest as:
- CLI commands that do not exist or have incorrect syntax
- References to vendor features that do not exist in the specified firmware version
- Incorrect IP addresses, VLAN IDs, or subnet masks
- Outdated security recommendations that do not reflect current best practices
- Invented RFC numbers or standards references
All AI-generated output must be reviewed and validated by a qualified human professional
before implementation. Nothing in this pack constitutes legal, financial, regulatory, or
professional engineering advice.
Authorized Use Only — Security Testing
You are solely responsible for ensuring that you have proper written authorization before using
any prompt outputs in security testing, vulnerability assessment, penetration testing, port
scanning, or network reconnaissance activities.
Performing these activities on networks, systems, or devices that you do not own, or for which
you do not have explicit written authorization from the system owner, may violate:
United States:
The Computer Fraud and Abuse Act (CFAA), 18 U.S.C. § 1030, which prohibits unauthorized
access to protected computers and carries civil and criminal penalties including imprisonment.
This is general information only, not legal advice. Laws vary by state. Consult a qualified US
attorney.
United Kingdom:
The Computer Misuse Act 1990, sections 1 (unauthorized access), 3 (unauthorized modification),
and 3ZA (unauthorized acts causing serious damage), which carry penalties including
imprisonment.
This is general information only, not legal advice. Consult a qualified UK solicitor.
Other jurisdictions:
Similar laws exist in Canada, Australia, the European Union, and most other countries. If you are
operating outside the US and UK, you are responsible for understanding and complying with
applicable law in your jurisdiction.
This product is designed and written for the US and UK markets. It does not constitute legal
advice in any jurisdiction.

United States:
